- **2026-11-19, step 9c: how readings are built.** Tahseen set what; these are mine, except where marked "decided by the coordinator, for Tahseen to confirm".
  - *One standing value:* the once-only index (`ux_observations_vital_sign_once`, name kept) no longer includes the visit, for every role. The vital signs page refuses a different value for a sign that stands at that time before saving, with "A value already stands for this sign at that time. Mark it entered in error first, then record the correct one." (the blood pressure's own wording); the database refuses it too.
  - *The visit, decided once, from the time it was taken, as Tahseen worded it:* `ehr.file_result` gives a vital sign the visit of the first value recorded for the patient at that time, standing or in error, whatever visit the client names. The first value takes the patient's latest held visit that had already begun when it was taken, within the twelve hours before (the statuses that count are step 9's), and never one that began after; a visit the client names for it must be that one. A lock on the patient and moment makes a second transaction for the same moment wait and then find the first. The first build kept step 9's fifteen minutes after the time taken, on the coordinator's word, so a set taken just before a check-in had no visit if typed before the check-in and the visit if typed after; the step's review showed that misses "from the time it was taken, not the moment the row is inserted", and the fifteen minutes went. **Corrected 20 November:** Tahseen restored them (his answers below): a sign taken up to fifteen minutes before a visit's start belongs to it, decided from the time taken alone, and a check-in ties the sets already recorded to its visit, so vital signs taken in the waiting room up to fifteen minutes before check-in are tied to the visit whenever they are typed. The seed, the test fixtures and the step 9 migration all take a visit's vital signs at the moment it began, which the rule ties to it unless another visit of the patient begins within fifteen minutes after (an open question below).
  - *Readings:* `observations.reading_id`, given by the database when the two values are entered in one transaction, whatever the app sends (it can't name another patient's reading); one systolic and one diastolic per reading by an index (`ux_observations_reading`); only blood pressure values have one (`ck_observations_reading`). `ehr.check_vital_reading`, run by three constraint triggers deferred to the commit, refuses a reading across two patients, times or visits, or with one value standing: a lone value can't be committed, and a correction can't join a reading in error. An update runs it only when the reading, patient, time, visit, or a value's status or code changes, so a migration that re-ranges readings (step 9d) leaves nothing waiting and can put the table's protection back in the same transaction. It runs as its owner, because forced row-level security binds the owner too and a check that saw nothing would pass, so the owner reads observations (`observations_owner_select`, as for patients and notes). The runtime roles can't skip it: `SET CONSTRAINTS` only moves it earlier, `session_replication_role` is the superuser's, disabling a trigger the owner's (all three tried in `VitalReadingSecurityTests`).
  - *Marking:* `ehr.mark_reading_in_error` marks the other value in the same statement, as the caller, with the same reason, each value its own audit event; it refuses if who is signed in changed after the first value was marked, so step 9b's rule for one row holds for the pair. The mark page lists a blood pressure as one item and sends the systolic's ID only: sending both made the second update find its row already marked and refuse the set, as the body mass index did in step 9b.
  - *Reads:* `BloodPressure` pairs a reading's two values by ID. The flowsheet has one row; the chart's newest blood pressure is one item, its up or down mark shown only when the two numbers didn't move opposite ways (decided by the coordinator, confirmed by Tahseen on 20 November); a visit's and the offline copy's come from one reading, with the desktop contract unchanged. The trends keep a chart for each number, fed only by whole readings: one combined chart would need a second series in the trend and its partial, and the portal's charts had to stay as they are.
  - *The migration (`VitalReadings`):* in order, the visits of each moment made its first value's (first values' visits are not decided again); duplicates marked "duplicate resolved by migration 9c" keeping the most recently entered; where that marked a weight or height, the body mass index standing beside it marked "body mass index of a value marked as a duplicate (migration 9c)" and one worked out again from the weight and height kept, as `ehr.file_result` works one out (a test compares the two); standing values whose partner is in error marked with Tahseen's reason; values that never had a partner marked "blood pressure value without its partner (migration 9c)" (decided by the coordinator, confirmed by Tahseen on 20 November; none in the seed, the test fixtures or the local stack); then the readings, a systolic and diastolic in error sharing one only when marked together (the same reason, by the same person, within a second), so no reason is hidden behind another. A row with no code is no sign, and is neither compared nor moved. The visit moves, the marks and the body mass index worked out again are audited (the moves too: **decided by the coordinator, for Tahseen to confirm**), with nobody signed in, so the audit page shows "System" and no one is recorded as marking; the readings are not. Each count is printed in the migration job's output: Npgsql logs notices only at the Debug level, which the bundle doesn't show, so the design-time context writes them out (`DatabaseNotices`). Rolling back drops the readings and keeps the marks and visits. On the local stack, counted read-only and not migrated: 0 visits to move, 0 duplicates, 1 value whose partner is in error, 0 without a partner, 292,737 readings.
  - *The seed* gives each visit's pair one reading and loads them with the readings' check off, as it does the audit trigger, and `SeedTests` checks every seeded reading instead (AGENTS.md says so). Left on, the check can't wait for the commit (PostgreSQL refuses the `ALTER TABLE` that puts a table's protection back while checks are pending), and run as each table loaded it added about a fifth to the time 50,000 patients take, over the tenth I had set as the limit.
  - *A refusal at the commit* is reached only by SQL past the pages: the vital signs page sends a blood pressure whole and refuses a second value first, and the mark page marks a reading by one value.
  - *Left open:* the visit rule is `ehr.file_result`'s, which runs for the apps' roles; an insert as the owner or a superuser can still give another sign of a moment another visit (within a reading the commit check holds it for every role). Two marks of one reading started from its two values at once can deadlock; the database refuses one, and the page, which marks from the systolic, never does it.
  - **For Tahseen,** not changed: "keep the most recently entered" goes sign by sign, so it can leave standing a pair that was never entered together. 120/80 recorded, then 130/85 typed whole after a check-in (the older rule let it stand, at the visit), then the 85 marked in error alone: the migration keeps 130 as the newer systolic and the 80, which still stands, as its diastolic, leaving 130/80. The alternative is to treat the 130 as a value whose partner is in error, mark it with that reason, and leave 120/80. Neither the seed nor the local stack has such rows (no duplicates on either). **Answered 20 November:** a reading must be a pair entered together, so the migration now leaves 120/80 and marks the 130 "not entered together with a standing partner (migration 9c)" (his answers below).

Rolling back gives the readings step 9b's five bands again.

- **2026-11-20, Tahseen's answers on step 9c.** His words:
  1. "Restore the allowance: a vital sign taken up to 15 minutes before a visit's start belongs to that visit. The visit is still decided once, from the time taken, whenever the value is typed. Add a test for a reading taken 10 minutes before check-in and typed after it, and one taken 20 minutes before, which stays unattached."
  2. "Never-entered-together pairs: a blood pressure reading must be a pair that was entered together. Where the latest values of each sign weren't entered together, keep the most recent pair that was, and mark the other values entered in error with the reason 'not entered together with a standing partner (migration 9c)'. Make it a rule in the code, with a test built from my example (120/80, then 130/85 typed whole after check-in, then 85 marked in error: the result is 120/80). No existing rows change, so no migration run is needed beyond the rule."
  3. "Your additions stand: the own reason for values that never had a partner, and recomputing body mass index when a duplicate weight or height goes. The chart's up/down rule for blood pressure stands too."
  4. "Cite the AAP table as the published article, Pediatrics 140(3):e20171904, Table 6, and add your note about the 'ROUGH GALLEY PROOF' text in the PDF's text layer beside it."
  - *1, as built.* Deciding the visit at a moment's first value alone would leave a set taken within the fifteen minutes and typed before the check-in without the visit for good, and give it the visit when typed after: typing order would still matter. So the check-in ties the sets already recorded to its visit (the coordinator's proposal): `ehr.attach_vital_signs` (`db/policies/75-results.sql`), after the insert of a visit the apps make, ties every vital sign of the same patient taken from fifteen minutes before its start to twelve hours after it, with no visit or the visit of one that began before it, to the new one: exactly the sets the rule now gives it. A moment moves whole, values in error too, so a reading is never split, and only its visit changes. Each value moved is audited, as the person checking the patient in: the write-audit trigger records every change to a PHI row, and a check-in's transaction has no way, or reason, to skip it. A value being recorded and a check-in for the same patient wait for each other, so none is missed. It runs as the tables' owner, with a policy of the owner's own to update observations (`observations_owner_update`, `30-rls.sql`); the apps' roles can't call it or change a visit. `ehr.file_result` gives a moment's first value the latest held visit that began no later than fifteen minutes after it was taken, within the twelve hours before. Tests: his two cases and their mirrors in the database and through the pages (VitalSignVisitSecurityTests, TC-UC4-25), the edge at fifteen minutes, a moment moved whole with nothing else changed, who can cause it, and a value and a check-in at once.
  - *1, after the step's review:* the first build trusted the visit's start and status as the app sent them, so a clinician with the patient on their panel could insert, past the pages, a visit dated two years ago and have the patient's two-year-old vital signs tied to it; before this step the apps could not change a vital sign's visit at all. Now a visit the apps insert starts at the database's wall clock, `clock_timestamp()`, whatever they send, as an appointment's `booked_at` and a result's `issued_at` do, and any status but in progress is refused (`ehr.check_in_visit`). The check-in is the only place the apps add a visit, always in progress, so the pages never meet the refusal; should one come, the appointment page shows its "Not saved" (it reached the clinician as an error page before the second review). The seed and the migrations insert visits as the owner. A first fix after the review held the start to within a minute of the transaction's start instead; the second review showed a session holding its transaction open could still backdate a visit by as long as it held it, so the database now sets the start itself, and there is no window to judge or clock skew to refuse. The tie uses the same start and the same window as `ehr.file_result`'s rule, fifteen minutes before it to twelve hours after, so a set the check-in takes is one the rule gives that visit, and the reverse. Two visits starting at one instant pick the one with the smaller ID, as `ehr.file_result` does; two the apps start never share an instant now, so the test sets the start rule aside to try it. **What remains of "decided once":** after a check-in has tied a moment, nothing the apps do moves it but a later check-in within fifteen minutes after the moment was taken, which the rule gives it to, and never back to a visit that began earlier; a value of the moment takes the moment's visit whatever it names; the owner and superusers can change anything, as always. The reviews' probes (a backdated visit, a transaction held open), the tie, and a moment on a later visit left alone are tests (`VitalSignVisitSecurityTests`, and `CheckInRefusedTests` for the page).
  - *1, existing data:* no migration. Read-only on the local stack, no set lacks a visit the rule now gives; 13 seeded moments sit with a visit the rule would now give to another (below).
  - **Open, for Tahseen:** the rule takes the latest visit that began within fifteen minutes after the time taken, as step 9's did. When a patient has two visits a few minutes apart, a set taken at the first visit's start belongs to the second. The seed makes such pairs: on the local stack, 13 seeded moments (three values each, taken 2 to 14 minutes before another visit of the same patient began) are tied to the earlier visit, which the rule now gives to the later one. Nothing moves them: the tie at check-in acts only on check-ins from now on, and the seed loads visits directly. If a visit that had already begun should win, the rule changes in `ehr.file_result` and the check-in moves only sets with no visit.
  - *2, as built:* in the `VitalReadings` migration itself, on the coordinator's instruction: it has run only on the local stack and in tests, and Tahseen said no rerun is needed. *Entered together* means written by one transaction. The data shows it as the values' insert audit events having the same time (the transaction's start) and person; `issued_at` can't show it, since `ehr.file_result` has always stamped each value with `clock_timestamp()`. Values with no insert event, which the seed and the `VitalSigns` migration loaded, were entered together when they share their `issued_at` (both give the visit's start). A moment's standing pair is the most recent pair entered together whose two values stand. Every other standing blood pressure value is marked: an older standing pair, "duplicate resolved by migration 9c"; where a pair stands, any other value, "not entered together with a standing partner (migration 9c)"; where none does, a value whose partner entered with it is in error, his partner reason, one with no value of the other sign, "blood pressure value without its partner (migration 9c)", and any other, "not entered together with a standing partner (migration 9c)". Other signs keep the most recently entered value. His example is a migration test and gives 120/80, the 130 marked; failure B (130 typed alone beside a standing 80) gives 120/80 too, the 130 marked.
  - **For Tahseen:** failure A, 120/80 with the 80 marked in error alone and 78 typed alone (step 9b's way of correcting one number), has no pair entered together standing, so under the rule nothing stands at that moment: the 120 is marked for its partner, the 78 as not entered together. The same for a systolic and a diastolic saved separately. Both values stay in the record with their reasons. Neither occurs on the local stack or in the seed, and step 9b's page never reached `main`. If such a moment should keep its latest value of each sign as a reading, that is one more branch of the rule.
  - *2, after the step's review:* values loaded with no audit event are entered together only when they also share the visit they were loaded for: two visits starting at one instant load two pairs with one `issued_at`, which the first build took for one group of four, no pair, and marked all four. Now the pair loaded for the visit the migration gives the moment (the visit of its first value, whatever sign that is) stands, and the other is a duplicate; between pairs both or neither loaded for that visit, the one with the smaller ID. (The first version kept the pair with the smaller ID alone, which the second review showed could keep another visit's pair when the moment's first value was a heart rate.) The visit a value was loaded for is the one it had before its visit was first moved, which every move records in the audit trail, so the migration run again after a rollback decides the same. A test shapes it. Read-only on the local stack, nothing changes by it.
  - **For Tahseen, not changed:** the reasons differ by what is left over. An older complete pair superseded by a newer complete pair is "duplicate resolved by migration 9c"; a single value left over is "not entered together with a standing partner (migration 9c)". The review's example: 120/80, then 125/82, then 130 typed alone: 125/82 stands, the 130 is "not entered together", and the 120 and 80 are "duplicate". If he wants one reason for every value the rule marks, it is a one-line change.
  - *2, existing data:* read-only on the local stack, which ran the earlier rule: 585,476 blood pressure values in 292,738 moments, each moment one systolic and one diastolic entered together (16 values have an insert audit event, the rest were loaded), and the one value the migration marked for its partner was entered with that partner. No row there would come out differently, so no rerun.
  - *3:* confirmed, and marked so in the step 9c entry above: the reason of their own for values that never had a partner, the body mass index worked out again when a duplicate weight or height goes, and the chart's up or down rule for blood pressure.

- **2026-11-19, step 11: Tahseen's decisions on the plan for the FHIR API,** in his words where they are rules.
  - *Vital signs:* "claim the FHIR vital-signs profiles instead of base R4. Send each blood pressure reading as one Observation with LOINC 85354-9 and systolic (8480-6) and diastolic (8462-4) components in mm[Hg], built from the reading's stored pair. Give every vital sign the vital-signs category, its LOINC code and UCUM unit, and validate against the profiles in tests. If the stored data can't reliably pair a systolic with its diastolic, stop and tell me." It couldn't until step 9c gave each reading an ID: see the next two entries.
  - *MRN:* "send it with the system http://swiftchart.example/fhir/sid/mrn and the identifier type MR (http://terminology.hl7.org/CodeSystem/v2-0203). Record the URI in docs/expansion/reference-data.md." A search takes the MRN alone or as that system|MRN.
  - *Thyrotropin:* "fix the seed's unit now (UCUM m[IU]/L) and re-seed, and add a test that every unit in the reference data and seed is valid UCUM."
  - *Sensitive items:* "Sensitive resources included through show-sensitive=true carry meta.security with the confidentiality code R (http://terminology.hl7.org/CodeSystem/v3-Confidentiality)."
  - *And:* "a capability statement at /fhir/metadata that matches what's implemented, errors as OperationOutcome, the application/fhir+json content type, no CORS headers, and the /fhir endpoints in the render benchmark."
  - *His picks among my questions:* the parameter is `show-sensitive`; "No known allergy" is a text-only AllergyIntolerance; an appointment's participants have the status `accepted`; medications entered in error are left out; a diagnosis has the category `problem-list-item`.
- **2026-11-19, step 11: pairing a blood pressure's systolic with its diastolic: found unreliable, then decided.** The stored data couldn't pair them: nothing tied the two rows but the patient, the time and a visit looked up afresh for each row as it was inserted, so neither "patient, time and visit" nor "patient and time" paired every reading the pages could produce (cases A, B and C, under Tahseen's step 9c entry above). Of the four ways forward offered (fix the cause first; pair by time now and leave out the doubtful; pair by time and visit; send the two values apart), Tahseen chose the first, done at the root: step 9c, where the database gives a reading's two values one reading ID and holds every reading to one standing systolic and one standing diastolic, or both in error. Until it was merged the API left blood pressure out and said so.
- **2026-11-19, step 11: a step 9b defect found, not fixed here.** A second, different value for the same moment is accepted when a visit began in between (case B above): step 9b's rule that "a different one is refused by the database" rests on the once-only index, whose key includes the visit, and `ehr.file_result` gives the later row the new visit. The vital signs page then shows one of the two. Fixing it is `db/policies` and a migration (way 1 above): step 9c did.
- **2026-11-19, step 11: the read-only FHIR API, as built.**
  - *Where and who:* `src/Ehr.Web/Api/Fhir` (`FhirApi.cs`, `FhirResources.cs`, `FhirObservations.cs`), at `/fhir` on both apps, with the Clinician policy on the whole group. The staff cookie is already the default for `/fhir`, so the signed-in clinician reaches the database as for a page; the cookie's refusals are a 401 or 403 there, as under `/api`, with an OperationOutcome (an authorization change: `AccountSchemes.FhirPath`, `AccountServiceCollectionExtensions`). The page error handler leaves `/fhir` alone; the API's own (`UseFhirErrors`) answers every refusal and failure with an OperationOutcome in fixed words.
  - *The gate* is the chart's: the patient on the clinician's panel, or emergency access in force. A search is gated on its patient before anything is read. A read by ID first asks the reader whose entry it is (the ID and nothing else, not a recorded read), then the gate, then the read; a patient's ID is gated first, so an unknown one is 403, as the chart sends it to the reason page. An off-panel patient found by MRN is 403, which is stricter than the search page.
  - *Reads:* new `PhiReader` methods, each one event per request: `read`, or `read_sensitive` when `show-sensitive=true` asked (the chart's event, which the cloud audit queue already takes). Each request runs in one transaction, from an endpoint filter, as a page's does under `DatabaseTransactionFilter`. A search costs fewer round trips than the chart.
  - *Searches* return at most 600 entries (what the vital signs page reads), the latest first, with `total`, and say what they left out in OperationOutcome entries. Each entry's address is built from `Ehr:Links:ClinicalBaseUrl`, never from the request (step 6c); the stack sets that to the off-site address for both apps.
  - *Codes:* as the record has them; units with UCUM's system and code only when the unit is its code's reference unit. Decimals are sent without the column's trailing zeros. Oxygen saturation claims the base vital-signs profile, since R4's `oxygensat` profile asks for LOINC 2708-6 and the record holds 59408-5: the API doesn't add a code the record doesn't hold. Say if 2708-6 should be sent beside it.
  - *Strict:* an unlisted parameter, a repeated one or an unusable value is 400; `_format` is taken for JSON. Any method but GET is 405 with `Allow: GET`. Off-site a write is forwarded like every write, so the client sees the clinic's server's 405, or the cloud app's plain "Not saved" (503) while that server is out of reach.
  - *Dependencies, in the web tests only:* Hl7.Fhir.R4 6.8.0, Hl7.Fhir.Specification.Data.R4 6.8.0 (R4's definitions, 5.8 MB, used offline), Firely.Fhir.Validation.R4 3.6.0 and Fhir.Metrics 1.4.0. All BSD-3-Clause (read from each package's nuspec and the validator's LICENSE file). Pulled in with them: Hl7.Fhir.Base and Hl7.Fhir.Conformance 6.8.0, Firely.Fhir.Validation and its Compilation package 3.6.0 (BSD-3-Clause); MessagePack.Annotations, Newtonsoft.Json, System.ComponentModel.Annotations and System.ValueTuple (MIT). About 17 MB to download and 55 MB unpacked. The restore's advisory check passes. The newest releases (6.9.0 and 3.6.1, published on 18 and 19 November) were passed over for ones a few weeks old. The app has no FHIR package: it writes the JSON with System.Text.Json.
  - *Thyrotropin in databases already seeded:* the reference table always had `m[IU]/L`, and results filed through the app take the reference's unit, so neither changes. Only the seed's own rows carry "mIU/L": `SELECT count(*) FROM ehr.observations WHERE code = '3016-3' AND unit = 'mIU/L'` finds them. The FHIR API sends them with the unit as text until they are re-seeded or corrected. The local stack is left to Tahseen.
  - *Not built:* paging, other types (Encounter, Practitioner, notes), `_include` and the like, history, XML, writes, OAuth or SMART on FHIR. A read can't start emergency access. Pages still show "m[IU]/L" as it is spelled (`Units.Shown` doesn't rewrite it).
  - *After the review:* a search by MRN no longer answers 403 for a patient off the clinician's panel. It answers as for an MRN that is nobody's, 200 with an empty Bundle and nothing recorded, so it can't tell anyone who is a patient here (the coordinator's choice; a mistyped MRN of one's own patient answers the same way). And every search is now newest first by its type's own date, undated last: medications, which came by name, by when they were started; the rest already were (allergies by when recorded, diagnoses by onset, results and vital signs by when taken, appointments by start). The capability statement says which date.
- **2026-11-20, step 11: the blood pressure panel, as built on step 9c.**
  - *One Observation for each standing reading,* in Tahseen's words "with LOINC 85354-9 and systolic (8480-6) and diastolic (8462-4) components in mm[Hg], built from the reading's stored pair". Its `id` is the reading's ID, which is stored, stable and unique: not a row's. It claims R4's `bp` profile, has the vital-signs category, the code 85354-9 with the text "Blood pressure", and its two values as components, each with its LOINC code, its value in mm[Hg] (UCUM's system and code, and the unit as the record stores it) and its own reference range, as the other vital signs send theirs. Like them it has no interpretation and no encounter. `effectiveDateTime` is the reading's time, both values'; `issued` is the later of the two values', when the reading was whole; the status is theirs.
  - *Paired only by the reading's ID, never by time.* `PhiReader.ObservationsAsync` reads a reading's two values in the same statement and pairs them by `reading_id`; the systolic is the entry, since a diastolic is no entry of its own, and the API makes a `BloodPressure` of the two, as the pages do.
  - *By ID:* `GET /fhir/Observation/{reading ID}` is the reading. A value's own ID is not found, and neither value of a reading is ever an Observation of its own (a row coded 8480-6 or 8462-4 with no reading, which only a laboratory row can be, is an Observation like any other: after the review). A reading in error is left out, like any value in error. The gate's question, whose entry it is, finds a blood pressure by its reading's ID, before the gate, as before.
  - *Searches* count a reading once, in `total` and against the 600, newest first as before. A request costs the statements it did: a search is the user context, the panel check (and the emergency-access check off the panel), one query, a count only when the limit cut the list, the audit insert and the commit; a read by ID adds the lookup. `FhirReaderTests` counts them: a reading read by its ID is one statement of observations and no count.
  - *Gone:* the "not served yet" note, from searches, the capability statement, the README and `docs/UC19.md`. The capability statement's `supportedProfile` gains `bp`.
  - *Tests:* the reading validates against R4's `bp` profile, and the same reading without its diastolic doesn't (the control); TC-UC19-01 and 02 read and search a reading, leave out one in error, refuse a value's own ID, and count the reads; a patient whose only vital signs are blood pressures gets one Observation for each reading. The benchmark's FHIR rows now go to the panel's patients who have a blood pressure reading, and its read by ID reads readings.
  - *After CI's benchmark* (run 44704492594, cancelled at 30 minutes; "FHIR observations" alone ran 16 minutes): every search and every read by ID read all the diastolics in the table. The left join's key had become the reading's ID and status, a guard added in review, and EF Core compares two such keys as LINQ does, matching a null reading ID to a null one: `a = b OR (a IS NULL AND b IS NULL)`, which no index answers. So the plan was the same with or without statistics: all 200,911 diastolics of the 50,000-patient seed, filtered for each row listed, 1.3 s for a search and 0.26 s for a read by ID. Held now with no join and no condition across two columns. A search reads the patient's own rows by the patient's index, both values of each reading among them, at most twice the 600 since an entry is at most two rows, and pairs them by `reading_id` in memory (statuses alike). A read by ID and the gate's lookup are each one statement, a UNION ALL of three lookups of a unique key (the primary key, with no reading ID; `(reading_id, code)` for the systolic, and for the diastolic), which the planner costs as one row whatever its statistics. The count, when the rows read may have been cut, is of one table. Each request sends the statements it did. `FhirPlanTests` EXPLAINs every query the API's reads send, on 10,000 seeded patients with autovacuum off, with the statistics cleared and then analyzed, and fails on a sequential scan of any table the seed fills; it fails on the old join. The benchmark stops a row that runs over a minute.

- **2026-11-20, step 11b: a child's screening value in the FHIR API.** Steps 9d and 11 were built on two lines and met on the integration branch, where every test passed and one thing was wrong that no test looked at: the API sent a child's blood pressure screening value as the `high` of a reference range. FHIR reads that as the top of what a value may be, so a reading equal to it would look within range, when step 9d's rule is that a reading at or above it needs follow-up. The API now sends such a range with no numbers and says it in the range's `text`, which R4 allows (a range needs a low, a high or text) and the validator accepts for the blood pressure profile. I chose words over R4's `referenceRange.type`, because none of its codes means "screening threshold" and I won't invent one. No `interpretation` is sent for any observation, as before.

- **2026-11-20, step 12: the simulated lab, as built** (new use case UC 20; proposed FR-54). From the coordinator's approved plan and its decisions: a migration (`LabKeys`), owner-owned functions that demand the key's hash, sex and age left out of the hand-over, and CI's two checks and the admin page's "Lab".
  - *The door:* `/api/lab` on the on-prem app only, mapped only when `Ehr:Lab:Key` is set: `GET /api/lab/orders?since={watermark}` and `POST /api/lab/orders/{id}/results` (`src/Ehr.Web/Api/Lab`). The key in `X-Lab-Key`, hashed and compared in fixed time first; a request that also carries any of the app's credentials (an `Authorization` header, a staff, portal or sign-in cookie) is refused. The database is given the hash of the key the caller presented. The cloud app maps none of it and forwards nothing under `/api/lab`. Requests with the key are rate-limited in their own window, everyone else's in a small one apart, so a keyless flood can't lock the lab out. Every answer is no-store JSON, failures included, in every environment; every refusal a fixed word and sentence. Times are read with a `Z` or any offset, never without one, and written in UTC with a `Z`. (After the review of the first series, which found: the shared window let anyone lock the lab out through the cloud site; a `collectedAt` with an offset failed; the database was given the app's own key's hash; a `null` result failed; a staff cookie beside the key wasn't refused; the developer page could show a failure in Development. After the second review: a time without an offset was read in the app's own zone; a sign-in cookie named in other letter cases beside the key wasn't refused.)
  - *The database holds it too* (`db/policies/76-lab.sql`): `ehr.lab_orders` and `ehr.file_lab_results` run as the tables' owner and demand the SHA-256 of the lab's key, found in `ehr.lab_keys` (no runtime grant), with nobody signed in; the migration job stores the hash from `EHR_LAB_KEY`, worked out in its container, or none. For them: five owner-only policies in `30-rls.sql` (lab orders only; a result of an order only; acknowledgments; diagnoses that aren't sensitive only), which hold only while `app.lab_door` is `open`, set by the two functions while they work and cleared before they return (after the review: before, they widened what any function running as the owner could see; a test keeps the list of functions that run as the owner and name these tables at the lab's two, since a session could set the value by hand and call one, and after the second review it reads SQL-standard bodies too, by what they depend on, and names in any case and spacing), and the filing trigger's condition in `75-results.sql` now also takes an app session's insert through a function that runs as its owner (`session_user`), so the lab's rows pass `ehr.file_result` as a clinician's do. Without the acknowledgment policy, that rule's check would silently have passed for the owner: a test holds it.
  - *The hand-over:* by transaction ID from the change log (lab orders' inserts, `20-change-log.sql`), whole transactions, as many as come to at most 100 orders, or one alone that signed more (the pages sign one a transaction). `signedAt` is UTC with a `Z` whatever the session's time zone (after the review). Each order's ID, signing time, the tests still waiting (an order part-filed by hand gets the rest) with unit, range and decimals, and the patient's current diagnoses that aren't sensitive. Each order handed over is a `sent_to_lab` audit event, recorded by the database in the same statement. Lab orders signed before this step are not in the change log, so never handed over. The watermark stops short of the oldest transaction still running on the server that has written something, so a session left open in a transaction holds back every lab order signed after its first write (documented for operators after the second review).
  - *Filing:* all or none and once, the order's row locked first; an identical repost is answered as the first; a different value for a filed test, a discontinued or acknowledged order, a test not reported, a wrong unit, a future collection: refused, nothing filed. The writes are audited with no actor and the source `lab` (`40-audit.sql`); the admin page shows "Lab".
  - *Bounded:* `RateLimits:LabRequestsPerMinute`, 120, for requests with the lab's key, and `RateLimits:LabKeylessRequestsPerMinute`, 30, for all others: at most 12,000 `sent_to_lab` events a minute while no transaction signs more than 100 lab orders (the pages sign one), and only with the key.
  - *The simulated lab* (`src/Ehr.Lab.Simulator`): HTTP only, no project of the app's, the contract's one file compiled in. Values from the clinic's ranges as handed over, moved by a small table of ICD-10-CM prefixes (E10/E11, E78, E03, E55, N18, D50 to D64), out of range one time in twenty otherwise; delays 5 to 30 seconds; the same order and settings give the same values and delay. No eGFR in the reference data, so kidney disease moves creatinine and urea nitrogen.
  - *The stack:* a `lab` service, on by default, and `EHR_LAB_KEY` optional. Unset, the app has no door, the migration job stores no hash, and the service logs one line and waits doing nothing: `docker compose up --wait` fails a stack where any service exits, even with 0 (checked with Compose 5.5.1 on a throwaway project), so exiting wasn't possible. `.env.example` has a placeholder key, so CI's stack runs the lab; its stack job asks the on-prem site with the key and the cloud site, and checks the service runs.
  - *The results page* says "Lab connected:" for an order still waiting when the database holds a key; the by-hand form stays.
  - **For Tahseen,** each built as the default shown: (1) when the clinician who signed an order can't be told (their account disabled) and nobody has the patient on their panel, a lab's result reaches no inbox; it is filed all the same (step 7's rule, unchanged). Should admins be told then? (2) A lab's results reach the portal at once, before anyone acknowledges them, as a clinician's filing does (step 7's open question, now for the lab too). (3) A lab's post that meets a different result entered by hand is refused whole, and the clinician's stands. (4) Lab orders signed before step 12 are never handed to the lab; they are resulted by hand.
  - **Open, for Tahseen:** set `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` for the app roles, so a session left idle in a transaction can't hold back the lab's hand-over for long (not set: a session left open holds it back until it ends).

- **2026-11-21, step 13a: visit summaries, as built** (proposed FR-55; extends UC 6, Simar's, and UC 14, Satwik's). From the coordinator's approved plan and eight adjustments, and Tahseen's answers to the plan's nine questions. A migration (`VisitSummaries`: the table and a new inbox kind; no data moves).
  - *What it is.* A visit summary is its own table (`ehr.visit_summaries`), never the note: what a clinician writes for the patient from a signed note in three parts ("What we talked about", "What we found", "What happens next"), each required and up to 4,000 characters, typed from nothing with the note under the form for reference. One stands per note. It is released, never edited; a wrong one is withdrawn with a reason. The portal reads summaries and never notes or addenda. Any clinician with the patient on their panel releases and withdraws one; the note's page says who and when. The portal dates each by the day its note was signed, as the results page dates a result ("Nov 2, 2026 UTC"), since nothing ties a note to its visit's own date.
  - *No internal codes.* One rule, held by the database (`ehr.visit_summary_problems`), which the page asks to say what it found when the rule refuses a summary (*after the review:* it asked first, so every release ran the rule twice): a UUID; the patient's own MRN; an ICD-10-CM, RxNorm (5 to 7 digits), LOINC or UNII code, or one of the clinic's own order codes, each only when it is a real code in the reference data, so doses, dates and words like "B12" or "N95" pass; and a whole section of the note of 40 characters or more. Its limit, in `docs/UC14.md`: a pasted fragment, a code typed another way, a category that isn't itself a code, and another patient's MRN get through. *After the review,* look-alikes are read as they look (characters that show nothing taken out, fullwidth and other compatibility forms folded, dashes read as hyphens); a letter of another alphabet that looks like a Latin one still gets through.
  - **Tahseen's decisions of 21 November 2026,** on the plan's nine questions, in his words: "Keep 1, 2, 4, 5, 6 and 7 as approved.

    3. Withdrawing: the portal keeps a placeholder where the summary was: 'A visit summary from <date> was withdrawn by your care team. Contact the clinic if you have questions.' No content, no reason. If the patient or a proxy had viewed the summary before it was withdrawn, send the patient the same generic email as a new summary. If the person withdrawing isn't the one who released it, the releaser gets an inbox item, as with another clinician's addendum. Add tests for the placeholder, the email only after a view, and the inbox item.

    8. Keep as approved, and add a step to the plan (after step 18): the admin audit view shows who did what to which record, but no clinical text for any patient data, sensitive or not. Note it in the phase 4 pull request draft.

    9. Change: a summary starts sensitive if its note or any of its addenda is sensitive at release, and the clinician can mark any summary sensitive. If the note or an addendum becomes sensitive after release, the summary becomes sensitive too, recorded as inherited, under the same inherit and unmark rules as notes and addenda. Add tests for both paths, including that a proxy without sensitive access loses the summary at once when the mark arrives."
  - *The nine, as built,* each with the alternative not taken:
    1. Sensitive summaries on the portal are left out until the patient, or a proxy allowed sensitive items, asks ("Show sensitive summaries", a link, so a read the cloud app answers too), and the asking is recorded as a view of sensitive items. Not built: showing them at once with a tag.
    2. A sensitive summary's release emails the patient like any other; the email says nothing of it. Not built: no email for a sensitive one.
    3. As he set out above (and below, "mine").
    4. Any clinician with the patient on their panel releases and withdraws, labeled with who did. Not built: the note's signer only.
    5. Only this patient's MRN is looked for. Not built: any patient's, which would tell a clinician whether a string is somebody's record number without a recorded read.
    6. A whole section of the note of 40 characters or more is refused, with case and runs of white space ignored. Not built: no such rule, or a stricter one on fragments.
    7. Each part up to 4,000 characters, all three required. Not built: a different limit, or optional parts.
    8. An administrator sees the text of a summary that isn't sensitive in the audit log's details, as for notes today (step 2b's open point), until step 18a; a sensitive one's never.
    9. As he set out above: sensitive at release when the note or any addendum is (inherited) or when the clinician ticks "Sensitive" (its own mark); afterwards inherited when the note or any addendum becomes sensitive (marked, or an addendum signed sensitive), cleared when none of them is; marked on its own with a reason, unmarked only by the person who marked it or the note's signer, and never while the note or an addendum is sensitive. Not built: sensitive with the note only.
  - **Mine, for Tahseen** (each built; say if you want it otherwise):
    - *"Viewed"* means the portal recorded a read of that summary by the patient's account or a proxy's. The portal page now records a read of each summary it shows, besides the one read of the patient (the plan had one event a view); a clinician's reading doesn't count. The database answers it (`ehr.withdrawn_summary_was_viewed`, owner-run) only to the clinician who has just withdrawn that summary, since clinicians can't read the audit log. *Corrected after the review:* "in the same transaction" was a check of the time and of who withdrew it, which a transaction of the same clinician's open since before another of theirs withdrew it also passed; it now checks that this transaction wrote the summary's row (its xmin is this transaction's, a savepoint's included). Its limits: a clinician can withdraw, ask and roll back, leaving no trace; and a view is inferred from audit rows that SQL run as the app's role, or the cloud app's shipper, can insert; either way the most it does is one generic email. A view made off-site counts once the cloud app ships it: within seconds while the link to the clinic is up, but while it is down the view waits until the link is back, so a summary withdrawn in between counts as not viewed and no email is sent (this entry said "every couple of seconds", which held only while the link was up). `docs/UC6.md` and `docs/UC14.md` say so.
    - *The withdrawal email* is not held back by the one-a-day rule for releases, and a release the same day is held back by a withdrawn summary as by any other (the rule counts summaries released earlier the same day, the clinic's day since step 13d's review round). It goes to the patient's own confirmed account only, whoever viewed it.
    - *The placeholder* takes the withdrawn summary's place by its date; a replacement of the same visit stands beside it, before it. A withdrawn sensitive summary's placeholder is shown only to someone who could have seen the summary, once they ask, and counts in the "hidden" number; a proxy without sensitive items gets none and no hint. It carries no "Sensitive" tag. The portal gets the placeholders from `ehr.withdrawn_visit_summaries` (owner-run: the date and the sensitivity, under the standing summaries' rule), never the row, because row-level security can't hide a row's columns. *Corrected after the review:* the standing summaries came through a policy for the portal, which gave a portal session the whole row, a mark's reason and who made it included; now no portal session reads the table, and the portal gets each standing summary's ID, date, writer, three parts and mark from `ehr.standing_visit_summaries` (owner-run, under the same rule).
    - *The inbox item* is a new kind, `summary-withdrawn` ("Visit summary withdrawn": "A visit summary you released was withdrawn"), with a link to the note and nothing of the summary or the reason, delivered by the database in the same statement (`ehr.deliver_summary_withdrawn`, owner-run); nobody is told when the releaser can no longer sign in, as for an addendum; the releaser marks it done.
    - *An inherited mark's reason* is the database's: "Its note or an addendum to the note is sensitive.", and when cleared "Neither its note nor any addendum to the note is sensitive now." *Corrected after the review:* it was the source's own reason, which carried the words given for the note or the addendum onto the summary.
    - *The release form doesn't offer the tick* when the note or an addendum is sensitive (the summary is sensitive anyway), as for an addendum to a sensitive note (step 10e's open point).
    - *On the note's page,* a sensitive summary of a note that isn't sensitive is left out until asked for ("Show sensitive summaries"), and showing it is the one recorded view of sensitive items the page records for its addenda too.
    - *Marking or unmarking a note or an addendum, and signing a sensitive addendum, now need READ COMMITTED,* as releasing a summary does, even for a note with no summary: under an older snapshot the summaries could miss a change. The apps use READ COMMITTED everywhere these happen.
    - *The summaries' update policy has no "who may unmark" clause* (the notes' and addenda's do): any panel clinician must be able to withdraw a summary someone else marked, so the trigger alone decides who unmarks. *Corrected after the review:* its check also required a summary withdrawn since the transaction began to have been withdrawn by the person signed in. That refused a legitimate change, a colleague's withdrawal committed while the signer's transaction was open making the signer's mark of the note, of an addendum or of the summary fail, and didn't do what this entry said; it is gone. The trigger sets who withdrew one; a statement that changes who is signed in part-way could record someone else, the same known limit as steps 9b and 10b.
    - *The clinician's chart* tags a note with a summary standing, "Summary released", from the notes query it already makes.
    - *Not built:* a FHIR resource for summaries (UC 19 is unchanged); summaries in the desktop app's offline copy, which already has the signed notes; the portal's app bar has no "Visit summaries" link (the home page has it).
  - *The owner's test* (`NoteSensitivitySecurityTests`, renamed `…_and_a_proxy_sees_no_note_or_addendum_and_its_summary_only_as_the_grant_allows`) keeps every assertion it made and adds the patient, a proxy without sensitive items and one whose access was removed, the summary in every count, and two proxies' real views of the summaries page that stay in the log after the mark.
  - *UC owners:* UC 6 (Simar): a page, a link on the portal's home and a section of `docs/UC6.md`; its existing dates and test cases unchanged. UC 14 (Satwik): pages, the note's page, the note sensitivity page's wording, and a section of `docs/UC14.md`. UC 4 (Diego): the chart's tag. UC 18: two lines of `docs/UC18.md`.
- **2026-11-21, step 13c: who viewed my record, as built** (proposed FR-57; extends UC 6, Simar's). From the coordinator's approved plan, with its notes on the emergency mark, names, inference by proxies and the page's own read. No migration: the index on the patient and the time (`ix_audit_events_patient_id_occurred_at`) already serves it. **Protected path: `db/policies`** (`42-record-views.sql`, new; in `30-rls.sql` one owner-only policy on `break_glass_accesses` and a sentence on who reads the log).
  - *The page:* `/portal/views`, "Who viewed my record", linked from the portal's home: each view of the record in the last 12 months, newest first, 50 a page, with "Older views" and "Newer views" as plain links that carry a moment and nothing else. Who: a staff member by the name and role their account has now, "You", someone the record is shared with by the address the patient gave access to, or "The lab" for a lab order handed over. When: "Nov 2, 2026, 14:05 UTC". Which part: "Your record" (with its sensitive items; downloaded, step 13b's two actions, named already so either lane can land first), "A visit note", "A visit summary", "A conversation in your messages", "A lab order, sent for testing", "Who viewed your record". Emergency access tagged, with a notice. Not cached; no ID anywhere in the markup. A proxy's address can be one long word, so a word too long for an `sc-list` line now wraps inside it (the shared stylesheet and the desktop app's copy); the Access page's list of ended access gains it too.
  - *The door:* `ehr.record_views(older_than, newer_than)` runs as its owner, answers only for `ehr.current_patient_id()`, takes no patient, and returns a time, words and marks: never an ID, a table name, an action, a reason or a write's values. Nothing else about `ehr.audit_events` opens to patients: its policies are the four it had, which a test holds. The function maps an event to a fixed part key in one place (`ehr.record_view_part`), and the page maps keys to words in one place (anything unknown reads as the record), so step 13c2 only adds a part to the log that wins over the table-and-action rule. Every table of patient data is named by that rule, and a test walks the list `ProtectionTests` keeps and fails for one that isn't, so 13c2 and later steps must decide each new one; a view of a table it doesn't name is the record to the patient and hidden from a proxy without sensitive items.
  - *Which part, sequencing:* most pages record a read of the patient's own row whatever part they showed, so the chart, results, vital signs and appointments are all "Your record" for now. The owner asked for "which part" in his own words, so this is sequencing, not a cut: step 13c2, in this phase and once steps 13a, 13b and 13c are merged, records which page was opened on each read, on a stable base, rather than three lanes editing `PhiReader`'s call sites at once. `docs/UC6.md` says most views read "Your record" until then.
  - *What a proxy can't infer:* the lines, where a page ends and whether there are more (`more`) are computed from the lines the viewer may see and from nothing else, because the filter is applied before the page is cut; a page ends on a whole moment, so the next page starts from a moment alone. Which tests prove which filter: the hidden-views paging test for a proxy without sensitive items pages their view, adds thousands of events they aren't shown (the patient's own and another proxy's, sensitive ones, notes, addenda, conversations, writes, off-panel staff views and views through emergency access, at the same moments as theirs and between them) and pages again: the pages are identical, so each of those filters, the care-team rule among them, works before the cut. The second, for a proxy with sensitive items, does the same with the patient's own views and another proxy's, the ones such a proxy isn't shown: it proves the who-can-see filter, which the first can't tell apart from the others (after the second review, which moved that filter after the cut and found a proxy with sensitive items given an empty page where 46 lines and `more` belonged, with every test green). Each test fails with its filter moved after the cut. What remains is time, and it is observable: a page that reads past many hidden events takes longer. The review measured 3.9 to 9.3 ms at the median for a proxy whose record holds 20,000 views they aren't shown, against 0.44 to 0.69 ms for an empty record, with no overlap, so a handful of requests tells "thousands of hidden views" from "none". What it tells is coarse volume only, never who looked, when, or at what; every probe is itself a view, which the patient sees on this page; and it can't be closed without a migration (an index that reads only the views a viewer may see, or a fixed cost per page). Accepted as a residual for Tahseen to review. The finer signal the second review found is closed: the panel history was read only when a staff member's view reached the care-team check, so a restricted proxy's page that showed no staff line but took the read's time (0.9 ms against 2.1 to 2.5 ms at 60,000 assignment rows) told them one off-panel view, perhaps through emergency access, lay in that range, and `newer_than` narrowed when. The history is now read for every such proxy's page, before anything else, whatever the page holds; a test counts the panel table's scans for a page with no staff view and one with forty hidden ones: one each. (The third check found the first build of that read slow at a clinic's size; decision 9 says what it cost and how it is guarded.)
  - *After the second review,* which confirmed the proxy rule's rows and pages and failed the round on one leak and one test gap: (1) the panel history's read told a restricted proxy, by time, of one hidden view (closed as above); (2) the who-can-see filter moved after the cut went unnoticed for a proxy with sensitive items (now proved as above). After the third check, which found the history's read slow at a clinic's size (decision 9), the guard changed from refusing to setting the time (decision 10), and a test now proves the twelve months bound what a page reads before it is cut (a record with more than a page of older views and three inside gives the three, with no more to come). Also: the care-team rule is proved at its edges for a restricted proxy (a view at exactly the start of an assignment shown, at exactly its end hidden, a microsecond either side, an assignment ended and begun again, two overlapping), each edge failing its test when moved; and the panel history is now guarded (decision 10). **For step 13c2:** an index on `patient_assignments (patient_id, clinician_id)` would make the history read and the emergency mark's lookup cheap; it needs a migration. The second review measured a clinician with 60,000 assignment rows and 14 lines inside emergency-access windows making the patient's page take 28 ms; at realistic sizes the mark costs 0.1 to 0.3 ms a line.
  - *After the review,* which found no leak through the function and failed the step on one conformance finding and one test gap: (1) a proxy without sensitive items was shown every staff member's view, emergency access included, where this entry, FR-57 and `docs/UC6.md` said the care team's only; the coordinator decided the rule in decision 9 below, built inside the filter before the page is cut, so pages, boundaries and `more` still depend on the visible lines alone (the hidden-views paging test now adds off-panel and emergency views; which tests prove which filter, above); the page tells such a proxy it lists the care team's views and says nothing of emergency access. (2) Three mutations of the emergency mark went unnoticed; tests now cover a clinician with no window reading inside another clinician's window, a window for another patient, a read at exactly the window's end, and a panel ended and begun again. Also from the review: the part rule names every table of patient data (`note_addenda` and `messages` were missing), a table it doesn't name is hidden from a proxy without sensitive items, and a test holds the rule to the list of tables; the emergency-access reason page and `docs/UC12.md` tell clinicians the patient can see that they used emergency access and when, never the reason.
  - *Gates:* see the step's log entry (left to the coordinator); the benchmark is left to CI, with rows "Portal who viewed, the patient's own" and "…, as a proxy", on-site and off-site, each record carrying 20,000 synthetic views over the year; since the review's rule, the proxy, who has no sensitive items, is shown none of their record's (the clinician's were made before the run put the record on their panel), so each of their pages reads the whole year, the most a page can read.
  - **Decided without Tahseen — to review** (each built as described; the alternatives not built):
    1. *Which part comes from what the log records today*, so most views read "Your record" until step 13c2. Not built in this step: recording a page-level part on each read (about 30 `PhiReader` calls, the cloud whitelist, the admin page, and exact-event tests across three lanes at once).
    2. *Emergency access is marked, never its reason*: the reason is free text written for the clinic's review and may name other people or carry staff-internal detail (principle 1). A staff member's read is marked when it falls inside one of their emergency-access windows for the patient and the patient was not on their panel at that moment, from the assignments' history; once they add the patient to their panel, their later reads in the window are not marked. Not built: showing the reason; a line for the `break_glass` event itself (not a view, and the read after it carries the mark); the window copied into SQL as 4 hours instead of the owner-only policy on `break_glass_accesses`.
    3. *Writes are left out*: the page lists views, and every page that writes was opened with a recorded read. Sign-ins, account and system events are left out; admins' reviews of the log record nothing, so none appear. Not built: "updated your record" lines.
    4. *A proxy sees the page under their grant, with staff members' views, the lab's and their own only* (which staff members' views: decision 9): never the patient's own views or another proxy's, not even as "someone else this record is shared with", which would tell a proxy another proxy exists, or when the patient reads the record. Stricter than the plan's first lean. The patient sees each proxy by the address access was given to, exactly as the Access page shows it, and nothing else of their account; not the name the proxy typed for themselves.
    5. *Sensitive items and messages:* a proxy without sensitive items is shown no view of sensitive items (`read_sensitive`, `download_sensitive`, or an event marked sensitive) and no read of a note, draft or dictation review (a read of a note already sensitive is recorded as a plain read of it, and notes never reach the portal); one without messages, no read of a conversation. Nor a view of a part the rule doesn't name: an unnamed table fails safe, hidden from such a proxy and the record to the patient. The patient, and a proxy allowed sensitive items, see them all. Not built: a per-note check, which would need the notes table in an owner-run function for no gain to a proxy, who never sees notes.
    6. *Volume:* individual views, 50 a page, over 12 months stated on the page; views by the same person of the same part in the same minute are one line with a count, on the page only, since a chart opening records one read per note listed and the summaries page one per summary. So a clinician opening the chart shows as "Your record" beside "3 visit notes" when three are listed, though none was opened: step 13c2 corrects that wording when it records which page was opened. Not built: one line per day (loses "when"); all time (an unbounded read when most views are hidden from a proxy); an event's ID in the paging link (whole moments instead).
    7. *The page's own read* is `read` of `audit_events` with no row, recorded once, after reading, so it lists from the next look on; the cloud app's queue takes exactly that shape. It can't be mistaken for a view of a visit summary (`ehr.withdrawn_summary_was_viewed` asks for `visit_summaries` and the summary's row). Not built: a read of the patient's row, which would read "Your record".
    8. *Names and roles as the accounts are now:* the role at the time isn't recorded; a disabled or renamed staff account is still named, by its name now.
    9. *Staff views from outside the care team are treated like views of sensitive items* (the coordinator's decision after the review, under the standing rule that the more protective option wins): an "Emergency access" line can tell a proxy of a crisis the patient chose not to share. A proxy without sensitive items sees a staff member's view only if that staff member had the patient on their panel at the moment of the view (the assignments' history, the moment test the emergency mark uses), so never a view marked as emergency access; plus the lab's and their own. A proxy with sensitive items sees every staff member's view, emergency access marked, as the patient does. The patient sees everything, unchanged. The patient's panel history is read once for every page of such a proxy, whatever the page holds (after the second review: it used to be read only when a staff view needed it, which the page's time gave away), and not for anyone else: the session's context is worked out once, by itself, and then one scan of the panels' table compares each row with the patient's ID, since no index finds one patient's ended assignments. That scan is the page's main cost for such a proxy. As first built for the second review, the context was written into the scan and worked out again for every row: the third check measured 553 to 650 ms at the median for a page at 60,000 assignment rows (3.6 s under auto_explain, 240,673 buffers); worked out once, 1.8 to 1.9 ms with no hidden view or one, and 2.5 ms with 1,000. A test reads the statement's plan and fails if any filter or index condition in it calls a function of the schema, and the benchmark's panels' table now holds 5,000 ended assignments so CI's proxy row would show such a cost. Not built: (a) every staff view for every proxy, with the requirement reworded to "the clinic's staff"; (b) hiding the emergency mark only, which still shows a proxy that someone outside the care team looked; (c) hiding off-panel views from every proxy, sensitive items or not, rejected because for a child's or a dependent adult's record the proxy is the only one who will ever look, and off-panel access is what this page exists to show them.
    10. *The panel history is guarded* (after the second review, which backdated an assignment as the app role and changed what a restricted proxy saw; audited): now that the history decides what such a proxy is shown, an assignment the runtime roles insert starts now, and open, and the one change it takes is ending, now, once (`ehr.guard_patient_assignment`, `42-record-views.sql`). "Now" is kept as the app stamped it when within a minute of the database server's clock, so it stays on the clock the app stamps views with; further off, it is made the database's clock, as a proxy grant's time is, rather than refused (the third check's decision, on its facts: Windows with WSL2 loses minutes of clock after sleep, so refusing would keep a developer from "Add to my patients", and in a clinic a refused assignment would force emergency access for every chart opened). Either way an assignment can't be dated back or ahead by more than the minute. A row inserted already ended, a change to its ID, patient, clinician or start, a second end, a moved end or a reopening are refused. Every path that writes the table was checked first: at runtime only Ehr.Domain's `PatientAssignments`, from the chart's "Add to my patients" and "Remove" on the on-prem app beside the database (off site, the cloud app forwards the write there), stamping the time from its own clock a moment before saving; the benchmark's setup through the same class; neither the desktop app nor the sync API holds or replays assignments; the seed writes none; tests write as the superuser or as the app role at the database's own time. So no legitimate path writes an earlier time on purpose, and the minute is for the two machines' clocks to differ by. Rows the schema's owner or a superuser writes aren't held to it. Not built: refusing a time out of tolerance, as first built (a slipped clock would block assigning, as above); a wider tolerance (five minutes, as the cloud audit takes), which leaves that much room to backdate and still refuses past it; the database setting every time itself, as it sets an appointment's booking time, which would put assignments on the database's clock and views on the app's, so a view made just after an assignment could fall before it; no guard, which leaves "SQL as the app role can rewrite who was on the panel" beside "SQL as the app role can name any user", the same class, audited.
    11. *Off-site views carry the cloud server's clock* (a residual the second review named, not verified): the care-team rule and the emergency mark compare a view's time with an assignment's and a window's, stamped on site. With the cloud's clock running behind, a view made just after an assignment ended could be stamped before its end and shown to a restricted proxy, and one made just after an assignment began could be stamped before its start and hidden; ahead, the other way round. With both servers on NTP the margin is milliseconds. Recorded, not closed.
  - *UC owners:* UC 6 (Simar): a page, a link on the portal's home, a section of `docs/UC6.md` and TC-UC6-40 to 50 (13b has 24 to 34); its existing dates and test cases are unchanged. UC 18: two lines of `docs/UC18.md`. UC 2 (Tahseen): a line of `docs/UC2.md`. UC 12 (Luke): emergency access is now visible to the patient, and to anyone they share sensitive items with, marked, without its reason; after the review the reason page (`Reason.cshtml`) says so to the clinician, and `docs/UC12.md` gains one sentence (a heads-up for Luke). The brand guide's portal rule now names the page as the one place the portal shows anything of the audit log.
- **2026-11-21, step 13c2: who viewed my record, which part, as built** (proposed FR-57; extends UC 6, Simar's). From the coordinator's approved plan and its four additions. One migration, `AuditEventParts`: the audit log's `part` column and its check, and the panel history's index `ix_patient_assignments_history`. **Protected path: `db/policies`** (`10-grants.sql`: the runtime roles read the new column; `42-record-views.sql`: the part named, a `sensitive` column, the pre-visit form rule, comments on the index).
  - *What it does:* every read the PHI reader records says which part of the record it was for, one of a closed list (`AuditParts`), from the page or API that read it; the cloud queue carries it; the admin audit page shows it after Row and filters by it; "Who viewed my record" names it, in one table of words (`ViewsModel.Parts`), to each viewer as they may be told it. Which lines a viewer sees is decided as in step 13c.
  - **Decided without Tahseen — to review** (each by patient safety and privacy first, then the course document, then the smallest choice that's easy to reverse; each built as described):
    1. *A column, not a part worked out from the table and the details.* The table can't tell the chart from results, vital signs, orders or appointments, which all read the patient's row; the details hold a write's values, which the runtime roles can't read and the cloud queue refuses on a read, and a page name there would have no check. Rejected too: recording a read of each row a page shows, which multiplies the events a page writes and changes the events the audit tests assert.
    2. *The chart's reads of the notes it lists stay rows of the log, with the chart's part, and the portal folds them into the chart's one line,* "Your chart: the summary page, which lists your visit notes". TC-UC2-07's events and every note test are as they were. Rejected: dropping the listed reads from the page in the database (it needs a way to tell a page's own read from the rest), and a line of their own beside the chart's (repeats it).
    3. *Counts only for a part whose page records one read a view;* a part whose page records several (the chart, a note's page, a note being written, the visit summaries, the printable record, the offline copy, a pre-visit form) is one line a minute without a count, which says "with its sensitive items" when any of its reads showed them. A counted part's views of sensitive items are a line apart. Rejected: counting every read (the chart with three notes listed would be "4 times").
    4. *A proxy without sensitive items reads "The record" for the lists, orders, the inbox, who the record is shared with, a note's pages, a note being written and refill requests,* which could hint at a change or an item kept from them; every view read so before this step. Since every new read has a part, such a proxy can tell by elimination that a "The record" line was one of those, or a read recorded without a part, though never which (accepted by the coordinator after the review). They are told the chart, results, vital signs, a search, a panel, a day, appointments, the portal's home, conversations (with messages), the offline copy, the FHIR API and their own portal pages. A proxy without messages reads "The record" for a conversation's page that is a read of the patient (a new message). Rejected: naming every part to every proxy, and hiding the untold parts' lines (fewer lines than before, for no privacy gained).
    5. *The check is left NOT VALID.* Adding it with a read of the whole log would hold every audit insert, on the clinic's server and the cloud copy, for as long as the read takes; every row there when it was added has no part, which it allows. Rejected: validating it in a second, non-transactional migration (EF Core's advice for such a step), for a proof of nothing.
    6. *The list is closed in the database's check,* so a new part is a migration and a decision, as a new table of patient data is. Refill requests (13d) and pre-visit forms (13e) are in it from the start so those steps need none. Rejected: a check of the key's form alone, which takes any word.
    7. *The admin page's Part column comes after Row,* so the cells the audit tests read side by side (Who, What, Patient, Row) are as they were; with a Part filter beside Action. No clinical text.
    8. *A lock timeout of 5 seconds for the migration,* which builds the index first and takes the log's lock last. While it waits for a held log, every audit insert, and so every page reading patient data, waits behind it, for at most those 5 seconds (an insert started one second in waited 4.1 s in the review), and so does adding a patient to a panel, whose table the index's build holds; then the migration job fails, and runs again.
    9. *From the coordinator's additions:* a part the page has no words for, as from a database newer than the app, reads "Your record"; a test fails when the code's list, the database's check, the cloud allow-list and the words disagree in any direction. A pre-visit form's view is never shown to a proxy without sensitive items, whatever its table (`42-record-views.sql`), and reads "Your pre-visit forms", or "Your pre-visit forms page, with the forms hidden" for a forms page that showed none (corrected after the replay's review, decision 11); refill requests read "Your refill requests". The migration is named `AuditEventParts`, since `AuditParts` is the list's name. A deploy migrates first, then starts the apps; an app older than the migration records no part, which the allow-list takes and the page words as before.
    10. *Step 8's order-safety function is replaced in place too* (UC 1, Tahseen's; `db/policies/61-order-safety.sql`, a protected path of that step). It was dropped and created again on every run of the policies, outside a transaction, so an order form asking it as the migration job ran failed ("cache lookup failed for function", seen in its new test). It is now dropped only when the function there has a result other than the one the file creates (compared as `pg_get_function_result` gives it: the one change of result in its history, the step's first build without `against_sensitive`), in one transaction, and replaced in place otherwise. Its arguments never changed. Rejected: leaving it for the owner, since the same test that guards the who-viewed function shows the defect.
    11. *On steps 13d and 13e (the replay):* a pre-visit form's view has one name, `forms`, the part its pages' reads are recorded with; step 13e's part for a read of the forms' own tables, `form`, is renamed to it (`RecordViewParts.Form` is `AuditParts.Forms`), so the list of parts and the database's check are as reviewed, and the view is never shown to a proxy without sensitive items by either route. Nothing records a read of the forms' own tables today; one recorded as a plain read would be worded as the page with the forms hidden, since only a read of sensitive items is marked sensitive (the last check found the first wording of this claimed otherwise). A view that showed forms reads "Your pre-visit forms"; a plain read of the part, which only a forms page that showed none records (the clinician's with the forms hidden, the portal's for a session that may not see them), reads "Your pre-visit forms page, with the forms hidden"; both are counted as views, "…, 2 times", never as forms (the replay's review found "A pre-visit form" and "2 pre-visit forms" said more than happened). The portal's forms page opened by a session that may not see forms still records the forms part, not the home's: it is what was opened, and such a proxy is shown nothing of it, as before. Of the other parts whose page can record a plain read while hiding sensitive content (the chart, the lists, the visit summaries, the printable record, refill requests, a note's pages, the download, the FHIR API), each plain read showed what its words name, the part without its sensitive items, and none was changed. Rejected: renaming the recorded part to `form` (it changes the reviewed list and check). A refill request is told, as a conversation is, only to a viewer whose access includes messages, which is how step 13d lets a proxy ask; to a proxy without sensitive items it is "The record" either way. The order form opened to answer a refill request (`?refill=`) records `refills`, not `orders`. A clinician who holds a form's alert without the patient on their panel reads its answers with the forms' part: the patient, and a proxy with sensitive items, see a pre-visit form's view, not marked as emergency access (they hold the item, no emergency window); a proxy without sensitive items sees nothing of it.
    12. *A list's reads are written in one statement* (after CI's benchmark failed the clinic's home: "Panel, 100 patients", p99 excluding the commit 20.4 ms). The part's check is prepared again by every INSERT, and the reader wrote a list's reads through the unit of work, one statement each: 100 statements for the home, each 12 to 14 µs dearer (measured on a batch of single-row inserts: 26 to 28 µs with the check, 14 without, 20 with its list written as one array literal), so the home's audit inserts went from about 1.7 to 3.1 ms at the median (the benchmark at 3,000 patients, before and after, twice each, alternating). The panel's own statements didn't move: they read the open assignments' index, 0.16 to 0.19 ms with or without a clinician's 60,000 ended ones, before and after, and the benchmark measures the home before it fills the history. The reader now writes a list's reads together, as it already wrote a visit summaries page's (`AuditRecorder.RecordReadsAsync` through `RecordAllAsync`): one statement for the list, in the page's transaction. Rejected: writing the check's lists as array literals (half the cost stays, on every statement), and dropping the closed list (decision 6).
  - *Tests:* TC-UC6-63 to TC-UC6-70; `AuditPartTests` (each page's and API's part on every read; the lists kept in step; an unknown part); `AuditPartSecurityTests` (the check; the migration on a log in use, and with the log held); `RecordViewSecurityTests` (every part to every kind of viewer; a part never shows a hidden view; the hidden-views pages with parts; both panel reads by the new index); `CloudAuditTests` (each shape with its parts, and refusals); `AuditLogTests` (the column and the filter). The words of TC-UC6-40 to 45, 48 and 50, step 13c's, now name the parts; none of their lines changed.
  - *After the review* (no change to what a proxy sees; failed on one defect, one untested rule and a coverage claim): `42-record-views.sql` dropped and created `ehr.record_views` on every run of the policies, so a page calling it as the migration job ran could fail ("cache lookup failed for function"); it is now replaced in place, and dropped only while it has step 13c's result, once, on the upgrade, when a call in that moment can still fail (`docs/UC2.md`). A test re-runs the file while both runtime roles call the function. The rule that a pre-visit form's view never reaches a proxy without sensitive items is now tested on plain reads and downloads too. The audit recorder refuses a read recorded without its part, and `AuditPartTests` has a row for each path a wrong part went unnoticed on. Elsewhere: `61-order-safety.sql` (UC 1) dropped and created `ehr.order_safety_warnings`, which the order form calls, on every run, outside a transaction, now fixed the same way (decision 10); and every policy file drops and creates its policies statement by statement, so a query in that moment sees no rows rather than an error (not changed).
  - *UC owners:* UC 6 (Simar): the expected words of TC-UC6-40, 41, 42, 44, 45, 48 and 50 changed, step 13c's cases, which now name the parts; none of their lines or what they show changed. UC 12 (Luke): `docs/UC12.md`, the admin audit page's Part column and filter. UC 2 (Tahseen): `docs/UC2.md`, the queue's parts and the deploy note.
  - *Left:* a chart that lists more notes than a page holds can show its line on two pages at the same minute, as step 13c's "N visit notes" could. The benchmark's figures are CI's.

- **2026-11-21, Tahseen's standing decisions.** His words are the section "Standing decisions" above, whole; each is recorded here by name and date so the log shows when it was given.
  - *Step 15,* dictation proposals beyond the note: approved as designed (drafts only, labeled as proposed, signed through the normal order and allergy flows with their safety checks, nothing placed or saved automatically, the same grounding checks; new wording for FR-14 to propose; eval extended with synthetic cases).
