Recommend she not have any blood drawn today."); (b) a heading line ended all reading after an instruction, the retraction cues included ("Order a CBC. Correction: Cancel the CBC."); (c) sentences two or more on were read only for a retraction ("Order a CBC. Recheck in one week. Nah."); (d) any instruction in the next sentence exempted it, even one not plain ("The cardiologist will order it with the echo."); (e) a "Heading: text" sentence was accounted for whole ("Assessment: labs unnecessary."); (f) before it only two cue lists matched ("Things she refused.", "what we're not doing"); (g) the remainder no list closes: a longer sentence whose words of refusal, doubt, done-already, reassignment or wrong patient no list holds ("She turned down the blood test.", "Her standing lab order covers it.", "Mixed up my patients here."), and speech-recognition slips ("declinned"). Three changes to the trick set passed the gate and the tests (a sentence deleted, a tolerated sentence made a control with the cap lowered, a reversing one added to a tolerated group with the cap raised). And the A1c commit's whole-word reading made items no one asked for ("vitamin B12", "T4", "vitamin K2") and left their digits unchecked ("vitamin B7" as "vitamin B").
  - **For Tahseen: the structural rule, measured, not built.** The rule that would close (g): a plain instruction is proposed only when every sentence within reach after it, and the one before it, is itself a closed form (a plain instruction naming another item, a step of the plan in a closed form, a heading, the end of the dictation). Measured on top of the rules as they stood, with a closed form for a step of the plan (a plan verb with a catalog item and its quantities, or with "in two weeks" or "to clinic", or "refer to" a specialty): the next sentence only, nothing before: 5 of the 63 right (5 of 24), 35 of the review's 183 proposed, 6 of its 54 controls; the next sentence and the one before: 2 right, 26 proposed, 6 controls; the rest of the paragraph and the one before: 0 right, 11 proposed; to the next of the note's own sections: 0 right, 5 proposed; to the end of the dictation: 0 right, 4 proposed. Today's rules, for comparison: 19 right, 159 proposed, 28 controls. Every variant takes the recordings under his floor of 14, because real dictation puts free sentences beside its orders ("Emollients twice a day to the shins.", "She is due for routine blood work, so...", "Return on December 17 with home blood pressure readings"). Whether to trade the floor for it is his decision; it is not built.
  - *What the round built* (`validation.py`'s header has each word for word; each **decided without Tahseen — to review**, with the alternative rejected): (1) no exemption from the negation, error and condition rule for a sentence that opens a step of the plan (rejected: keeping it, which let (a) through); (2) the sentences after an instruction are read past heading lines, "Plan:" included, and the retraction cues reach past them; the reading stops only at the heading of another of the note's own sections (Subjective, Objective, Exam, Examination, Observations, Vitals, Assessment, Impression) (rejected: stopping at any heading line, as first built; and never stopping, which reads an Assessment's findings written after the plan as about the order: "Assessment: No acute distress."); (3) every sentence within 25 words, up to one holding an instruction of its own, is held to the rules the first is (rejected: the first alone, as first built); (4) what follows "Heading:" must be accounted for too (rejected: the heading accounting for the sentence whole, as first built); (5) before it, a retraction in the sentence right before it ("Hold off on these for now.") and a word of refusing, removing or putting off anywhere in its paragraph within 25 words ("Things she refused.", "Skip these today.", "what we're not doing"), unless said of what came before it ("Skip that.") (rejected: the two lists alone, as first built; and a retraction anywhere in the paragraph, which held back d50's lisinopril for the sed rate's "Skip that." two sentences earlier); (6) an instruction of its own, of any form, still ends the reading after an instruction: the review's ruling that only a plain instruction naming another item may end it was measured and not built (rejected for now: it holds back none of the review's sentences the other rules let through, and loses five of the recordings' right proposals, d47's levothyroxine, d50's CBC and lisinopril and d51's two allergies, each followed by an instruction that isn't plain, 18 to 13, under the floor of 14); (7) a word of letters and digits is read whole only when the whole word is a common name ("A1c", "HbA1c") (rejected: every such word, as first built); (8) the gate records the trick set's hash and fails on any other file, and on a control not proposed (rejected: counts kept in the file itself, which an edit changes with it); (9) the review's sentences are in the trick set, those the rules still propose in groups marked still_proposed, with no tolerance given, so the gate fails on them (rejected: tolerating them, or leaving them out); (10) the "CRP" name lets FR-14's rule list a long order list over it, kept and tested (rejected: keeping common names out of the listing, which would leave "and CRP" in the note).
  - *After the round, scored again (no model):* of the review's 183, 89 are still proposed (88 reversing, and the condition), nearly all longer sentences whose words no list holds; by the review's own families: a longer next sentence of refusal, removal or doubt 35, a step of the plan next 11, the common names 9, an instruction of another form next 8, done already 7, the wrong patient 6, given to someone else 5, speech-recognition slips 5, a heading next 2, two sentences on 1; of its 54 controls 25 are proposed, and the 29 held back are recorded as what the strict rule costs. The recordings give 18 right proposals of the 63 and of the 24 the rule can take (19 before): d49's latex at hvac 10 dB is lost, its next sentence heard as the fragment "formoterol and losartan.", short and not accounted for. The trick set, 590 sentences with the review's, gives 96 wrong proposals against a cap of 7, 88 of them reversing the meaning, so the gate fails, as it must while they are proposed; every control is proposed. Lists can close more of (g), as the round's did for (f), but not all of it.
  - *Known limit:* a common name decides whether a proposal appears, and, as any orderable, how far FR-14's rule lists a long order list that names it ("... and an iron panel and CRP" is listed and cut whole; tested); the forms search the reference data as before, so typing "CRP" in the order form finds nothing ("C reactive" does). His idea of putting the heard words into the search box would meet it.
  - **For Tahseen:** **EKG is not added:** no orderable exists for it in the reference data (LOINC's lab orders hold none; LOINC 11524-6 "EKG study" exists but isn't in the catalog) or among the clinic's common items, so the order form couldn't order one either; adding one is catalog work (a migration for a common item, or a source that names it as an orderable), not a name. Left as catalog work for later, each standing for more than one item or said in no plain sentence in the test set: sulfa (sulfonamides: sulfamethoxazole, sulfadiazine and others), hepatitis B surface antibody (four LOINC tests), vitamin B12 level ("level" is no word of its name), and the urine albumin to creatinine ratio (34535-5 and others); and the rest of step 15's list (sed rate, ESR, PSA, flu, strep, echo, LFTs, colonoscopy, DEXA, shellfish, MRI, mammogram and urine culture alone, plurals, classes, pollen, mold, cats, Keflex).
  - *Ideas for later, not now (his):* putting the heard words into the catalog search box (search only, never selecting); biasing speech recognition toward the catalog's names.

- **2026-11-24, Tahseen: step 15.2 withdrawn; step 15.1 lands with a cap in place of zero on the trick sentences** (FR-14 reworded, its row under "Requirements to propose"; extends UC 10, Samir's, UC 13, Satwik's, UC 1, Tahseen's, and UC 4, Diego's). His words (2026-11-24, about 13:20 CST), verbatim: "Change of plan: withdraw step 15.2 before it starts. I'm keeping the proposal feature for the demo. Record in the decision log that I withdrew the 15.2 ruling today, and why: proposals stay, with a cap in place of zero on the trick sentences, because no rule reached zero and the feature is wanted for the demo." Step 15.2 was his ruling of the same midday (item 1 of his rulings then, recorded in the log): give up proposals as a claim and list every instruction with a button to its form. It was planned and sized, never built, and the plan is kept outside the repository as a record. Instead, in his words, "land step 15.1 as built (the strict rule and its review round), tagged expansion-step-15.1", with four changes, built by a developer agent on the step 15 branch after the integration branch was merged into it. No migration, no model run, **no protected path** (not the dictation prompt or note schema, not `db/policies`, no authorization attribute or policy, not the sync conflict resolver).
  - *The conflict at the head of step 15.1's entry is resolved by the cap.* His two requirements, no proposal that reverses the meaning and at least 14 right on the recordings, couldn't both hold. The first is now a cap at what step 15.1 left, measured again on this branch with its checks before anything was accepted: the 590 trick sentences give 96 wrong proposals, 88 of them reversing the meaning, 89 of them among the 183 sentences step 15.1's review wrote (its 88 reversing and the one condition; the plan's count at step 15.1's head) and the other 7 the sentences tolerated by kind; every one of the 38 controls is proposed, and 29 plain instructions are held back by the strict rule, what it costs. Each of the 96 is accepted by name, in his name, dated 2026-11-24, with his reason in his words ("The trick sentences still proposed (the review's 89, and the rest of the 96 wrong proposals) are accepted by name in my name, as a known limit"), under `[accepted]` in `eval/trick-sentences.toml`, and `most_wrong` is 96. He gave the acceptances in writing; a developer agent wrote them on his instruction, the one time an agent writes an owner's acceptance, as the file says. The gate fails when a trick sentence not accepted by name is proposed, when the wrong proposals number more than 96, when a control isn't proposed, or when the recordings give fewer than 14 right proposals (they give 18); each of the four has a test that fails it and passes once put right. The eval gate passes, for the first time since step 15.1 began.
  - *What was heard after each proposal, beside its button* (his second change: "Each proposal shows the sentence heard after it, up to the next instruction or a heading, or a link to its place in the transcript, whichever is smaller to build, so a retraction is in view beside the button"). The sentence itself, never a link: no case needs one. The sidecar finds it in one place, `validation.heard_after()`, and sends it with each proposal as `heardAfter` (a place in the transcript, never words): the next sentence whole, then the sentences after it up to another instruction or the heading of another of the note's own sections, however far that is (the review round took away a limit of 25 words, which left a refusal said three sentences on out of view). The review shows those words under "Heard after it:", between the words as heard and "Open in the order form" or "Open in the allergy form", as said and never judged, and so does the form's notice. Every proposed trick sentence shows all that was said after its instruction's sentence but one control, whose next line opens the note's Assessment; so each of the 88 accepted sentences that reverse the meaning has its retraction there. A contract file written by the sidecar's real code from one of them ("Order a CBC. She turned down the blood test.") is staged by a web test and rendered by a browser test at 1,280 and 640 pixels in both themes, the refusal in view beside the button without a click.
  - *Everything else as built* (his third change): the empty forms, "As heard:", the common names, line breaks and headings, and no proposals for visits; `ehr.ai_chart_proposals` stays in use, and the dictation results keep their proposals key.
  - *Merged first:* the integration branch (step 16 and the plan, at 6274603) into the step 15 branch at ba1cb59, seven files conflicting, each resolved keeping both sides; and one name defined twice once merged, `_CONDITION` in `validation.py` (step 15.1's pattern of condition words and step 16's pattern text), which broke the proposal rules on any sentence after a plain instruction: step 15.1's is `_ANY_CONDITION`. Step 15.1's test cases TC-UC10-13 and 14, which step 17 also numbers, are TC-UC10-20 and 21.

The hashes, before (the integration branch at 6274603) and at this series' head: the dictations' structuring 108605225034, pipeline 8abf7e660584 and recordings 58ec8499a961 unchanged; gold 9387c74ab394 to b8a36a520a49 (step 15.1's gold); scoring 423cb1cea793 to 2a23e15f2574. The visits' structuring 22df8dc63212, pipeline 029c8d618a97, recordings 5b4a690da0f9 and gold 044123a6b09a unchanged; scoring 50da7915b0c2 to 1ce24671389f.

Say plainly that some retracted instructions can still be proposed, and say what was measured, with no estimate beside it (his ruling later the same day, verbatim: "Say instead what was measured: 96 of the 590 trick sentences are still proposed, 88 of them reversing the meaning, all accepted by name; the rate on real dictation has not been measured." The estimate first asked for was one reviewer's judgement from before step 15.1's review round, not a measurement, and he dropped it from the draft and the plan). Beside it, as he asked, two limits of what the review shows after a proposal, both following from where the reading stops: a retraction said after another instruction isn't shown ("Order a CBC. Her husband drove her in today. Her mother came along as well. Refill her lisinopril. She declined the blood draw." proposes the CBC with no refusal beside it), and neither is one under a new section heading ("Order a CBC.", then "Assessment:", then "She declined the blood draw."). The reviewer's estimate in step 15's entry above ("what the rules can't promise") stays there as the reviewer's words at the time; it is superseded by the measured trick-sentence counts and isn't quoted in the phase 5 draft.

(8) No longer open: the unit of the reviewer's estimate was put to him, and he dropped the figure from the draft and the plan (above).
  - *The review round* (2026-11-24, an independent reviewer: merge after fixes; each fixed, with tests that fail and pass): (1) what was heard after a proposal stopped after 25 words, the hold-back rules' reach, so in "Order a CBC. Her fatigue started around the middle of summer with poor sleep and low appetite. It continued through the fall with trouble concentrating at work on most days. She turned down the blood test." the CBC was proposed and the refusal wasn't shown, while the review says "Each shows what you said after it": the limit is gone from `heard_after()` (the rules keep it), which reads on to the next instruction or another section's heading, or the end; the longest span is the rest of the transcript after the proposal's sentence, at most what three minutes of dictation holds (2,392 characters in the test set's longest; 148 on the 100 recordings), shown as one wrapped paragraph above the button; (2) both results files were scored again from the run's own files (the model session's, a53b66b), not from files already scored again, so `rescored_from` names the run's scoring, as `eval/README.md` asks; nothing else in them moved; (3) the acceptances hold no slack in the gate itself: it fails when a sentence under `[accepted]` is no longer proposed, and when `most_wrong` isn't their number; (4) the trick sentences' proposals are counted, not the sentences: a control is proposed only at every instruction it lists (two list two), and the cap counts the wrong proposals (96 from 96 sentences).
  - *Heads-up for teammates:* Satwik (UC 13: "Heard after it:" on the review), Tahseen (UC 1: the order form's notice), Diego (UC 4: the allergy form's notice), Samir (UC 9/10: the sidecar's `heardAfter` and its contract file, the trick sentences' acceptances and the gate's four checks, TC-UC10-20 and 21).

- **2026-11-24, step 18: session timeouts and emergency-access review, as built** (proposed FR-63 and FR-64; new UC 24, "Review an emergency access"; extends UC 11 and UC 12, Luke's, UC 15, and, for recordings, UC 9 and UC 10, Samir's, and UC 23). Built by a developer agent from the coordinator's approved plan (decisions D1 to D16, questions T1 to T4) with Tahseen's changes to it, in a clone of its own, and applied here as patches. Six commits: the server's session times, the page's warning and end, the reviews in the database, the review pages, the benchmark's rows, and no-store. One migration, `EmergencyAccessReviews` (one table; no row moved). **Protected paths: `db/policies`** (`10-grants.sql`, `30-rls.sql`, `42-record-views.sql`, `70-inbox.sql`, and the new `72-emergency-access-review.sql`), and how a sign-in stays valid (`SessionTimes.cs`, `CookieSecurityStamp.cs`, `AccountServiceCollectionExtensions.cs`, the session pages' place in `PortalSubject.cs`). No authorization policy or attribute changed: the review page is under the Admin folder's policy and the session pages under their area's, by the existing conventions. The dictation service, its prompts and its note schema are untouched; `dictation.js` and `ambient.js` only say on the page when something is being recorded, so no eval hash moved.
  - **Tahseen's answers on the plan (24 November 2026), in his words:**
    1. "Add both of your cuts to step 18: a 12-hour limit on any sign-in, staff and portal, regardless of activity; and no-store on every signed-in page. Measure the benchmark after no-store."
    2. "The 15-minute idle timeout, staff and portal, with a warning 2 minutes before: fine, with two conditions." "Recording counts as activity. A dictation or a visit being recorded or uploaded (step 16's visits run up to 20 minutes with no typing) must keep the session alive, and the warning must never interrupt or end a recording. Add a test that a 20-minute visit recording isn't signed out." "A sign-out never loses typed text. Note drafts already autosave; for any other form, say what happens to what was typed. The warning's "Stay signed in" keeps the session without reloading the page."
    3. "Desktop idle lock: add it to the plan as its own step, before step 20." It is step 19a.
    4. "Emergency-access reason: stays visible to the reviewing admin after step 18a, as the plan assumes." Step 18a's text says so.
    5. "Review outcome: if it's small, a review records a required outcome ("appropriate" or "not appropriate") with its note, and the review list can filter to "not appropriate". If it needs more than a column and the page, list it for later instead." It was a column, the review page's two choices and the audit page's filter, so it was built; what follows a "not appropriate" is listed below, for later.
  - **Agreed by Tahseen on 2026-11-24** (two edges of his ruling 2, as the coordinator put them to him and he agreed them):
    1. *A paused recording may be warned; the warning says the recording so far will be lost if the session ends, and "Stay signed in" keeps it.* Built but for its last clause, which can't be true: see 17 below.
    2. *Starting a dictation or a visit is refused with a "sign in again first" message when less than that recording's maximum length remains before the 12-hour limit.* Built as agreed: 409 `sign_in_ending` when less than 3 minutes (a dictation) or 20 (a visit) is left, "Sign in again first: a sign-in lasts at most 12 hours, and this one ends in … minutes, too soon for a visit, which can record for 20 minutes. Sign out, sign in again, then start listening." (or "…for a dictation, which can record for 3 minutes. … then dictate."). An earlier build also kept 5 minutes for the draft; that was taken out to match the rule. A recording that ends close to the 12-hour end may then still be drafting at it: the page keeps the session alive while it drafts, but nothing moves the 12-hour end, and the dictation API refuses the page's next request (401), as it does any request after a session ends. What the page keeps then is not covered by a test.
  - **Known limits and follow-ups:**
    - *Where typed text is still lost.* What was typed is kept in the page's memory for the same account (below), but not when the tab or browser is closed (only browser storage would survive that, and it would hold PHI on the device), when someone else signs in on that browser (cleared on purpose, so they never see it), with JavaScript off (nothing runs to keep it; the server's end still applies), or for a proxy whose access ended meanwhile (the text was about a record they may no longer see). The pages written without the layout (the cloud app's "Not saved" page, the error pages) aren't kept either, and the desktop app is unchanged until step 19a.
    - *What follows a "not appropriate" review* (T3): telling the clinician, a conversation, the patient under breach rules. Not built; the review page and the audit page's filter find them.
  - **Decided without Tahseen — to review** (the plan's decisions as he amended them, and what the build added):
    1. *15 minutes idle for staff (was 30) and the portal (was 20), warned 2 minutes before, on both sites and for every role* (D1). *Rejected:* keeping 30 and 20; 10 minutes, which interrupts reading in a visit too often; a shorter time on-site or off-site, since network position is never identity; a setting per clinic.
    2. *The end is exact* (D2): any request renews the cookie once a minute has passed since it was issued, so the end is 15 minutes after the last request, to within a minute. The account is still checked every 5 minutes (`CookieSecurityStamp`): that check used to wait on the cookie's issue time, which a renewal once a minute keeps moving, so its own time is now kept in the cookie, and a disabled account loses even a busy session within 5 minutes. *Rejected:* renewing on every request (a `Set-Cookie` on every response for nothing).
    3. *12 hours, whatever the activity* (D9, his ruling 1): `CookieSecurityStamp` ends a sign-in 12 hours after it began; a sign-in from before this step counts from its first request since. No recording is started that the end would cut off (the agreed edge 2, above). In the last 15 minutes the warning says the end is final and offers "Keep working" instead of "Stay signed in". *Rejected:* cutting a recording at the 12-hour end; 8 hours, shorter than a shift.
    4. *What counts as activity* (D3, his ruling 2): any request made with the session; on the page a key, a click or tap, the wheel or typing, sent as a keep-alive at most once a minute, never mouse movement; and a recording. While a dictation or a visit is being recorded, sent, transcribed or drafted, the page marks `data-session-recording="recording"` on its body, set by `dictation.js` (its recording buttons) and `ambient.js` (start, resume, stop and draft, try again); the warning then never opens and the page never ends, and the page itself sends a keep-alive every minute, so a 20-minute visit with no key pressed, or a dictation whose audio isn't getting through, is never signed out (TC-UC11-09, the 20-minute visit run in Chromium with a moved clock). A visit paused, or waiting for its voices or for the clinician after a failure, is `waiting`: it may be warned, saying what the service does with it (above). *Rejected:* excluding recordings' requests; counting mouse movement (a nudged desk).
    5. *The script asks the server before it warns and before it ends* (D4), with a question (`/session`, `/portal/session` with the `Ehr-Session-Check` header) that never renews, in durations, so the device's clock never decides; tabs tell each other through a `BroadcastChannel` (one a kind, staff or portal), which stores nothing: one tab's keep-alive closes another's warning, and signing out in one ends the others. The page carries nothing of the session's own, no account, record or time left: the script asks for them as the page loads, so two requests for a page give the same markup and a portal page holds no identifier. *Rejected:* a readable cookie with the end; `localStorage` events; the account, the record and the time left in the page's markup (an identifier on every portal page, which its security tests refuse).
    6. *The warning is a modal dialog* (D5, a new `sc-dialog` component in the guide's style): the focus on "Stay signed in", Escape stays, "Sign out now"; the countdown shown, not announced each second; when something was typed, it says that it stays if the person is signed out. "Stay signed in" sends a keep-alive and closes it: the page isn't reloaded, so what was typed and where it was scrolled stay (TC-UC11-08). *Rejected:* a band at the top (out of sight on a scrolled page); a countdown read aloud.
    7. *A sign-out never loses typed text: the page is kept in the tab's memory behind a sign-in* (his ruling 2, replacing D6). At the end, or at a deliberate sign-out with something typed, the page's content is taken off the screen, title and all, into the script's memory, and "Signed out:" says why, with "Sign in again". Signing in again, in a frame on that page or in another tab, brings the page back exactly as it was for the same account, and its forms post as before; a proxy's page first chooses the same grant again, since every portal sign-in starts on the account's own record. Anyone else signing in clears what was kept and goes to their own home page. A page with nothing typed and nothing recording is reloaded instead, a new recorded read. Note drafts are on the server as typed anyway (TC-UC11-11 proves it at a timeout). `docs/UC11.md` lists every form and what can be typed in it; none is left out. *Rejected:* `localStorage`, `sessionStorage` or IndexedDB (PHI written to the device); a readable cookie; signing in again through the script's own requests (a second sign-in path beside the pages, the second factor included); a popup or a new tab for the sign-in (blocked popups, and the page left behind); keeping a server-side draft of every form (a table and an endpoint per form, for what the tab's memory does).
    8. *Without JavaScript* the server's end still applies, with no warning (D7).
    9. *The desktop app is unchanged* (D8): its token lasts 30 days; its idle lock is step 19a.
    10. *No page served to someone signed in is cached* (D10, his ruling 1): `NoStoreFilter` sets `no-cache, no-store` on every page and fragment for a signed-in session. A full page was already sent that way, by its sign-out form's token (ASP.NET Core's antiforgery sets it on any page with a form); the fragments a page asks for as someone types (the patient search's results, the code search) were sent with no cache header. `no-cache` with it, so the form token has nothing to override and logs nothing. *Rejected:* relying on the script (a cached page could flash before it runs).
    11. *One review an access, by an administrator who isn't its clinician, once it has ended, with a required outcome and a note of 10 to 500 characters, final* (D11, his ruling 5). *Rejected:* reviewing while in force (the review would miss what came after); reviewing one's own; an optional note.
    12. *A restricted table of reviews, keyed by the access, with no write-audit trigger* (D12): the row is its own record; administrators read and add, nobody else reads, nothing changes or deletes one, the owner included. Its migration's Down refuses once anything has been reviewed. *Rejected:* columns on `break_glass_accesses` (administrators would read and update a PHI table, and the clinician would see the note); a PHI table (administrators read none); an audit event alone (nothing would make it once).
    13. *Every administrator but the clinician gets an item, the review closes all of them, nobody ticks one off, and the migration job sweeps* (D13): the accesses from before this step, administrators added since and any failed delivery reach the inbox at the job's next run. The delivery never refuses the access: a failure is swallowed and leaves an audit event, `review_items_failed`, with the access, the clinician and the error's code and nothing else, and a warning in the server's log (the coordinator's condition; tested by making the delivery fail). *Rejected:* one administrator by rotation; items only from now on; a trigger on staff accounts for each new administrator.
    14. *What the reviewer sees* (D14, his ruling 4): the clinician, the patient by ID, when it began and ended, the reason, and that clinician's events on that patient while it lasted (time, action, table, row, part, site; the first 100), never an event's details or a value from the record, through `ehr.emergency_access_for_review`, an owner-run function that answers an administrator alone. Nothing read there is a recorded read of the patient. *Rejected:* hiding the reason; showing written values; an administrators' policy on `break_glass_accesses`.
    15. *The audit page* (D15, his ruling 5): "Review" stays on an access waiting, tinted; once reviewed, untinted, "Reviewed", and "Not appropriate" beside it when so; "Open the review" on each; and a Review filter for the accesses waiting or reviewed as not appropriate. *Rejected:* a list page of its own (the inbox is the queue, the audit page the whole list).
    16. *Off-site* (D16): the review page is read from the cloud copy and "Mark reviewed" is forwarded; cut off, the form gives way to a sentence and the "Not saved" page shows the note typed.
    17. *"Stay signed in" doesn't keep a paused recording; the warning says so* (the agreed edge 1's last clause, decided without him under his ruling of 2026-11-25 below). The dictation service discards a session no audio has reached for 10 minutes (`idle_seconds`, step 16), a visit waiting for its voices to be named too, and nothing reaches it while a visit is paused (step 16: "nothing is sent until Resume listening"). The warning comes after 13 minutes with no request at the earliest, and the last request is at least as late as the last audio, so when it opens a paused visit is already gone at the service, whatever the session does: "Stay signed in keeps it" would tell the clinician something false. The warning says instead: "The visit on this page is paused, or waiting for its voices to be named. The dictation service discards a visit left like that for 10 minutes, whether or not you stay signed in." Two ways to make it true, follow-ups, not owed work: (a) the service keeps a paused visit longer: the page tells it, through the web app, when a visit is paused, and the service's sweep gives a paused visit a longer limit than the session's 15 minutes and its warning (`settings.py`, `sessions.py`), its audio kept in the service's memory that much longer; about half a day, with the sidecar's and the web app's tests; (b) the page reaches the service while paused: every few minutes a request with no audio, through a new route of the web app, to a new call of the service that only marks the visit as still wanted, which relaxes step 16's "nothing is sent until Resume listening" to "no audio is sent"; about half a day to a day, with the consent-first and pause tests. Either changes the dictation service, which this step's brief kept out of bounds. *Rejected:* the warning promising what the service won't do.
    18. *The TC-UC23-06 limit test, a follow-up beside step 16's TC-UC23-02* (`AmbientBrowserTests.TC_UC23_06_a_minute_before_the_limit_and_at_it_the_status_line_says_so_once`): no more investigation, as decided without him under the same ruling. Since commit 2 (session.js) it failed in 11 of 29 full-suite runs on this Mac, each time waiting 30 seconds for the voice list after Stop, among them twice in a row at commits 4 and 6 of the final gate on 9192c1a; it passed in the 3 at commit 1, and never failed in isolation (8 runs, some on a loaded Mac) or in its class's run. That leans toward the series, so a cause in it isn't ruled out. Found: no path from session.js into the recording's uploads, its clock or the voice list; the page's only requests while recording are one session check as it loads and a keep-alive a minute. The likelier cause, unconfirmed: the test's fake recorder makes a chunk every 60 ms, which `ambient.js` uploads one at a time and must all send before Stop asks for the voices, so on a loaded Mac the backlog can outlast the 30-second wait. A side-by-side run against the base was swamped by fixture start timeouts and is inconclusive. The test isn't changed.
  - **Not covered by the tests:** the browser tests run in Chromium only, so Safari's and Edge's handling of the dialog, of timers in a tab out of sight and of the back-forward cache is unverified (WebKit, the macOS desktop's engine, included). The local stack wasn't run in the step's lane; CI's stack job runs the migration job with `72-emergency-access-review.sql`.

    - *The defect, fixed:* in a sign-in's last 2 minutes, "Keep working" and Escape only closed the 12-hour warning; the next 5-second tick asked again and opened it again, so a clinician finishing a note at 11 h 58 min could type about 5 seconds at a time, keys landing in the dialog (the reviewer's browser probe: open again after 5.3 seconds), against decision 3 and `docs/UC11.md`. Now a 12-hour warning once dismissed isn't opened again for that sign-in, and the end still ends the page. TC-UC11-14 (Chromium): it opens once in each page, Escape and "Keep working" each keep it closed through the later ticks, typing lands in the page, and the page ends at 12 hours with what was typed kept.
    - *Also fixed, from its remarks:* a cookie written before this step kept the old rules: a renewal keeps a ticket's own life (30 minutes for staff, 20 for the portal), and the account check's fallback to the issue time moved with every renewal, so a busy old cookie of a disabled account wasn't checked again until its 12-hour end. Now such a ticket's end becomes its issue time plus the idle time, and a ticket with no check time is checked at once (`CookieSecurityStamp.cs`, a protected path; `SessionTimeTests`: an old-shaped ticket idles out at 15 minutes, and one of a disabled account is refused at its next request). And the plan's "the dialog says it couldn't reach the server", which hadn't been built and wasn't logged: "Stay signed in" with the server unreachable now keeps the warning open and says the server couldn't be reached, to try again (TC-UC11-15), rather than closing and opening again at the next tick.
    - *Known limits, from its other remarks:* a session the server ends mid-recording leaves the page on screen until the page's own estimate runs down, up to about 13 minutes (`ambient.js` stops on its 401); a page restored from the back-forward cache after another account signed in isn't compared again (unverified; pages sent `no-store` are mostly not restored); the "Signed out" screen hides the page, but the tab's memory still holds it, readable with developer tools at an unlocked workstation, by his ruling that a sign-out never loses typed text; the sweep's lock test (`EmergencyAccessReviewSecurityTests.cs`, lines 261 to 296) would pass without the lock it tests, because running the file's `CREATE OR REPLACE TRIGGER` again blocks first (the lock itself is right); and `ix_audit_events_break_glass` is created without `CONCURRENTLY`, so it blocks the audit log's writes while it builds at a deploy.
    - *Its read of the flaky limit test (item 18):* no path from session.js that a clinician could see; 0 of 3 against 11 of 29 is weak evidence.

- **2026-11-25, step 19: passkeys as a second factor for staff, as built** (proposed FR-67; extends UC 11, Luke's, and UC 2's off-site table, Tahseen's). Built by a developer agent, tier A, from the coordinator's approved plan (one plan review, as ruled) and its four adjustments, in a clone of its own, and applied here as patches. Commits: the passkeys' table; the settings and Identity's handler; the passkeys page; the passkey at sign-in; a locked account's code refused (an adjacent security fix, below); the administrator's reset removing passkeys; the benchmark's row; this plan. One migration, `StaffPasskeys` (20261125183406, after step 18a's `UnroutedResults`; one table; no row moved). **Protected paths:** `db/policies/10-grants.sql`, and how a staff sign-in is checked (`StaffSignIn.cs`, `StaffPasskeys.cs`, `PasskeySettings.cs`, `AccountServiceCollectionExtensions.cs`, `ProtectedTokenStore.cs`, `StaffAccounts.cs`, the code page and its model, the passkeys page, `Program.cs`'s settings check). No authorization policy or attribute changed: the passkeys page is under the Clinical area's Staff policy by the existing convention, outside its anonymous Account folder.
  - **Tahseen's decisions (2026-11-25), in his words:** "19: 6a, 7a with user verification required (PIN or biometric), 8a, 9a." And: "Same rules as the rest of the plan: one plan review and one final review per step, decide anything new by the standing principles and log it, and the 8-hour box." What the numbers mean, as put to him: 6a, staff only, a passkey as an alternative to the authenticator code after the password, not passwordless; 7a, every staff account keeps its authenticator app, the passkey the quicker choice; 8a, tied to the parent domain both staff sites share, set in configuration, so one passkey works on-site and off-site, and changing the real domain means registering again while the authenticator still works; 9a, the desktop app keeps the password and the authenticator code. His rule for steps 18 to 21 (2026-11-25, recorded above) governs the rest: "Decide anything new by the standing principles and log it; come to me only for what's on the "what still stops" list." Nothing on that list came up: no library was added (Identity's own passkey support, and `System.Formats.Cbor` from the shared framework in the tests only), and no account or money.
  - **What ASP.NET Core Identity 10 gives,** read from the installed reference assemblies (10.0.12) and the 10.0.13 packages: `IUserPasskeyStore<TUser>` and `UserManager`'s passkey methods (add or update, list, find by credential, remove, a DELETE), Identity's `IdentityUserPasskey<TKey>` with its data in one JSON column (what `IdentityUserContext` maps from schema version 3; `EhrDbContext` maps it itself, for staff only); `IdentityPasskeyOptions` (the server's domain, user verification, "required" by default and enforced at both ceremonies, attestation, timeout, and an origin check that by default only compares the ceremony's origin with the request's `Origin` header); `IPasskeyHandler<TUser>` (`PasskeyHandler`), which makes the options and checks a passkey made or used, its signature, the person verified, the origin and domain, and the count. `SignInManager`'s own passkey methods keep their state in Identity's two-factor cookie, which this app doesn't register, and sign in without the password, so the app calls the handler directly and keeps the state itself. What the framework leaves to the app: the pages, where the state lives, the script that calls `navigator.credentials`, the origin list, the lockout and the audit.
  - **The coordinator's plan review** (2026-11-25): approved, with the adjacent lockout fix included as its own commit, the whole suite at three commits, and four adjustments: (a) bind the ceremony's state to the site's own session of it, for a sign-in to the password step; (b) the DELETE grant, with a security test that only the features that need it have DELETE; (c) a sentence on the passkeys page and in UC 11 that removing a passkey ends no session, and that an administrator's reset does; (d) Tahseen's by-hand check in `docs/UC11.md`. All four are built.
  - **Decided without Tahseen — to review** (each with what was rejected):
    1. *Identity's handler, called directly* (rejected: `SignInManager`'s passkey methods, which need Identity's two-factor cookie and sign in without the password; a third-party library, which nothing needed).
    2. *The ceremony's state travels in the page, protected by Data Protection for the authenticator's timeout and a minute, bound to its purpose, the account and this sign-in, or for a sign-in to this password step* (rejected: inside the `StaffPending` cookie, whose reissue could stretch its 10 minutes, or the Staff cookie, whose reissue would restart the 12-hour clock; a table for a 5-minute value; memory, with two apps). Adjustment (a): `HoldAsync` puts a random value of its own into the `StaffPending` ticket as it issues it, once per password step, and a sign-in's state carries it, so a state from one password step is refused under another of the same account; an add's state carries the Staff ticket's sign-in time (`SessionTimes`), which every renewal keeps. Known limit, as Identity's own design has it: someone holding both a pending cookie and the passkey answer made for it could send that answer again within those minutes; a passkey that keeps a count refuses it, a synced one (count 0) doesn't.
    3. *One table, `ehr.staff_user_passkeys`, in Identity's own shape, for staff only; the patient store's passkey type mapped to no table, so a passkey call on a patient account throws.* Internal, like the other account tables: grants only, no row-level security, no write-audit trigger (the app records the events). `ehr_app` gets SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE (DELETE because removing a passkey deletes its row, as step 16's transcripts are the precedent for); `ehr_read` SELECT (adjustment (b): a security test holds that only those two tables allow DELETE, both to `ehr_app`, and that `ehr_read` writes none of this one) (rejected: row-level security, which a sign-in with nobody signed in would have to pass, and which adds no bar where `ehr_app` already updates any account's password hash and tokens; a removed-at column, which the store would keep offering; an owner-run removal function, more protected surface for the same trust).
    4. *Passkeys off without `Ehr:Passkeys`, outside Development; checked at startup when set* (rejected: a required setting, which would stop every deployment and the CI stack for a convenience).
    5. *An exact list of the staff sites' origins; cross-origin frames refused; the portal's address never on it* (rejected: Identity's default, which would take a ceremony relayed from any host under the parent domain). A staff sign-in at the portal's address gets the code only.
    6. *The person verified at both ceremonies (7a), set explicitly; attestation "none"; any kind of authenticator; synced passkeys allowed* (rejected: device-bound passkeys only, which would refuse a Touch ID passkey in Safari, iCloud-synced; checking attestation, which needs FIDO's metadata, a dataset with an account).
    7. *Identity's default for a discoverable passkey ("preferred").*
    8. *A current code from the authenticator app to add a passkey, never a recovery code; a wrong one counts towards the lockout* (rejected: being signed in alone, which lets anyone at a workstation left signed in add a passkey of their own and later need only the password; a recent sign-in, which that workstation would pass).
    9. *At most ten passkeys an account; a name, required, at most 50 characters, never in the audit log.*
    10. *No security-stamp change when a passkey is added or removed,* as Identity does (rejected: a change on removal, which would end the person's desktop app and their own session for nothing a passkey holds). Adjustment (c): the page and UC 11 say that removing a passkey ends no session, and that an administrator's reset does.
    11. *An administrator's "Reset authenticator" removes all of the account's passkeys too,* each recorded as the administrator's doing; a passkey is never left on an account without its authenticator (rejected: a separate "Remove passkeys" action, which the reset covers; keeping passkeys at a reset).
    12. *The audit log names a passkey by the first 12 hex characters of a hash of its credential's ID:* `passkey_added`, `passkey_removed`, `passkey_refused` (adding: "wrong code", "not accepted"), a `sign_in` whose details name it, a `sign_in_failed` "passkey refused". They are about no patient, so the administrators' audit page shows their details since step 18a: the hash, nothing else (rejected: its name, free text; two events for one sign-in).
    13. *A passkey refused by the server counts towards the lockout as a wrong code does; a prompt cancelled never reaches it and doesn't count; a locked account's passkey is refused before it is checked.*
    14. *Off-site both posts are forwarded; the clinic's server checks the passkey and writes its count;* cut off, no sign-in, as with the code (UC 2's existing limit).
    15. *The script uses the browser's WebAuthn JSON helpers; a browser without them, or without JavaScript, gets the code only* (rejected: converting base64url by hand, more script for browsers that still have the code).
    16. *"Use a passkey" first on the code page, with the focus, the code always below; failures said in an alert, the focus kept; no prompt on load* (Safari needs a gesture). It is a plain button, not a second primary.
    17. *A "Passkeys" link in the staff app bar, where the clinic has passkeys.*
    18. *After a change of domain, old passkeys are refused and stay listed until removed.*
    19. *No "last used" date:* the audit log has every use.
    20. *The rate limits unchanged:* both passkey posts are under `/sign-in`, so a passkey sign-in is three posts where a code's is two.
    21. *An adjacent security fix: a locked account takes no code* (its own commit; the coordinator's instruction on the plan). Since the staff sign-in was built (main's security foundation, pull request 12, October 2026), the code step accepted a correct code from an account that wrong codes had just locked: within the `StaffPending` cookie's 10 minutes, someone with the password could go on guessing codes past the lockout, held back only by the per-client limit on sign-in posts, and a correct guess signed in, until the account check refused the session up to 5 minutes later (row-level security refused the locked account's rows meanwhile). Now the code, like the passkey, is refused while the account is locked, with the password page's words, and recorded ("locked out"); a test shows the right code refused, which signs in without the fix. The desktop app's sign-in was never affected: its password and code come in one request.
    22. *Chromium's virtual authenticator for the browser tests, through a test host that makes its app once its own address is known; a software authenticator for the server-side tests* (rejected: a fixed test port; a test-only setting in the app).
  - **One existing test's expectation changed:** TC-UC23-06's width check in the browser (`AmbientBrowserTests`) looked for any element past a 360-pixel window's edge; with the "Passkeys" link, a clinician's app bar navigation, a row of its own that scrolls sideways by design on a narrow window (`ehr.css`), runs past it. (The series said an administrator's already did; the final review found an administrator-only account's fits, and the test's comment was corrected.) The check now leaves the app bar's navigation out; its check that the page never scrolls sideways is unchanged (rejected: wrapping the staff navigation like the portal's, a change to every staff page at phone width and to the desktop app's copy of the stylesheet; hiding the link on a phone, where a phone may be the passkey being added).
  - **Known limits:** the replay in 2 above; Identity's authenticator code can be used again within its 30 seconds, as before; removing a passkey ends no session (10); the tests run Chromium and a software authenticator, so Safari's, Firefox's and Edge's own prompts and a real device are unverified until Tahseen's check; not walked on the local stack.
